. We refer to the vertices of
H^{\prime}
by the indices from
[\ell]
.
Consider the graph
H_{j}=H-V_{j}
: we are going to show that it satisfies the inductive hypothesis for
d-1
.
Clearly, the sets
\{V_{i}\mid i\in[\ell],\,i\neq j\}
form a partition of
V(H_{j})
; they are connected and non-empty.
For
i\neq j
, we obtain an
(\mathcal{H},k)
-separation
(C_{i}\setminus V_{j},S_{i}\setminus V_{j})
weakly covering
V_{i}
in
H_{j}
. (Recall that
\mathcal{H}
is hereditary.)
Finally, the quotient graph, obtained from
H_{j}
by contracting the sets in the partition, is
H^{\prime}-j
.
The elimination forest of
H^{\prime}-j
obtained by removing the root from the elimination forest of
H^{\prime}
has depth at most
d-1
.

By the inductive hypothesis, we obtain an
\mathcal{H}
-elimination forest of
H_{j}
of depth at most
(d-1)\cdot k
.
The graph
H-(C_{j}\cup S_{j})
is a subgraph of
H_{j}
, so we can easily turn the
\mathcal{H}
-elimination forest of
H_{j}
into an
\mathcal{H}
-elimination forest
(T_{j},\chi_{j})
of
H-(C_{j}\cup S_{j})
.
We start constructing an
\mathcal{H}
-elimination forest of
H
with a rooted path
P_{j}
consisting of vertices of
S_{j}
, in arbitrary order.
We make the roots of
(T_{j},\chi_{j})
children to the lowest vertex on
P_{j}
.
It remains to handle the connected components of
H-S_{j}
lying inside
C_{j}
.
They all belong to
\mathcal{H}
and
N_{H}(C_{j})\subseteq S_{j}
so we can turn them into leaves, also attached to the lowest vertex on
P_{j}
.
The depth of such a decomposition is bounded by
k-1
(the number of edges in
P_{j}
) plus
1+(d-1)\cdot k
, which gives
d\cdot k
.
∎

Given an
(\mathcal{H},k_{1},k_{2})
-separation decomposition of
G
, we can directly
apply

4.10
with
k=k_{2}
.
This concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 4.11
.

Suppose there exists an algorithm
\mathcal{A}
for
(\mathcal{H},h)
-separation finding
running in time
f(n,t)
.
Then there is an algorithm that, given graph
G
with
\mathcal{H}
-elimination distance
k
,
runs in time
f(n,k)\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
, and returns an
\mathcal{H}
-elimination forest of
G
of depth
\mathcal{O}(h(k)\cdot k^{2}\log^{3/2}k)
.
If
\mathcal{A}
runs in polynomial space, then the latter algorithm does as well.

Proof.

It suffices to process each connected component of
G
independently, so we can assume that
G
is connected.
By Lemma
3.5
, we know that
\mathbf{tw}_{\mathcal{H}}(G)\leq k
so we can apply
Lemma
4.5
to find an
(\mathcal{H},k,h(k)+1)
-separation decomposition
{T}
in time
f(n,k)\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.
If
\mathcal{A}
runs in polynomial space, then the construction in Lemma
4.5
preserves this property.
The graph
G\,/\,T
is guaranteed by Lemma
4.8
to have treedepth bounded by
k+1
.
We run the polynomial-time approximation algorithm for treedepth, which returns an elimination forest of
G\,/\,T
with depth
\mathcal{O}(k^{2}\log^{3/2}k)

[
34
]
.
We turn it into an
\mathcal{H}
-elimination forest of
G
of depth
\mathcal{O}(h(k)\cdot k^{2}\log^{3/2}k)
in polynomial time with Lemma
4.9
.
∎

By replacing the approximation algorithm for treedepth with an exact one, running in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(k^{2})}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}

[
97
]
, we can improve the approximation ratio.
We lose the polynomial space guarantee, though.

Lemma 4.12
.

Suppose there exists an algorithm
\mathcal{A}
for
(\mathcal{H},h)
-separation finding
running in time
f(n,t)
.
Then there is an algorithm that,
given graph
G
with
\mathcal{H}
-elimination distance
k
,
runs in time
\left(f(n,k)+2^{\mathcal{O}(k^{2})}\right)\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
, and returns an
\mathcal{H}
-elimination forest of
G
of depth
\mathcal{O}(h(k)\cdot k)
.

4.1.2
Restricted separation

To compute tree
\mathcal{H}
-decompositions, we will follow the global approach of Lemma
4.11
.
An analog of Lemma
4.8
also works to bound the treewidth of a quotient graph
G/T
in terms of
\mathbf{tw}_{\mathcal{H}}(G)
, as will be shown in Lemma
4.16
.
However, the provided structure of a separation decomposition turns out to be too weak to make a counterpart of Lemma
4.9
work for tree
\mathcal{H}
-decompositions.
This is because the separations
(C_{t},S_{t})
given by property (
3
) may intersect each other, which can cause the neighborhood of a set
V_{t}\setminus S_{t}\subseteq C_{t}
to be arbitrarily large even though
|N_{G}(C_{t})|\leq k_{2}
by property (
2
). We therefore need a stronger property to ensure that
|N_{G}(V_{t}\setminus S_{t})|
does not become too large.

Definition 4.13
.

We call an
(\mathcal{H},k_{1},k_{2})
-separation decomposition
restricted
if it satisfies the additional property: for each
t\in V(T)
there are at most
k_{1}
ancestors
s
of
t
such that
V_{s}\subseteq C_{t}
.

When building a separation decomposition by repeatedly extracting separations, this additional property states that a vertex set that eventually becomes the separated
C_{t}
-side of an
(\mathcal{H},k_{2})
-separation cannot fully contain too many distinct sets
V_{s}
handled by earlier separations.
Intuitively, if we find an
(\mathcal{H},k_{2})
-separation
(C_{t},S_{t})
that covers
V_{s}
for
s
being an ancestor of
t
, then we could have used it earlier to replace
V_{s}
with a larger set.
However, we have no guarantee that an
(\mathcal{H},k_{2})
-separation covering
V_{s}
would be found when processing the node
s
, but it can be found later, when processing
t
.
Since
V_{s}
is guaranteed to be
(\mathcal{H},k_{1})
-inseparable, this can only happen if
k_{1}<k_{2}
.
Unfortunately, we cannot enforce
k_{1}=k_{2}
, mostly because our algorithms for finding separations are approximate. However, we are able to ensure that the number of such sets
V_{s}
is small.

We introduce a restricted version of the
(\mathcal{H},h)
-separation finding
problem, tailored for building restricted separation decompositions.

Restricted
(\mathcal{H},h)
-separation finding

Parameter:

t

Input:
Integers
k\leq t
, a graph
G
of
\mathcal{H}
-treewidth bounded by
t
, a connected non-empty subset
Z\subseteq V(G)
, a family
\mathcal{F}
of connected, disjoint,
(\mathcal{H},k)
-inseparable subsets of
V(G)
.

Task:
Either return an
(\mathcal{H},h(t))
-separation
(C,S)
that weakly covers
Z
such that
C
contains at most
t
sets from
\mathcal{F}
, or conclude that
Z
is
(\mathcal{H},k)
-inseparable.

We are ready to present a general way of constructing a separation decomposition, restricted or not, when supplied by an algorithm for the respective version of
(\mathcal{H},h)
-
separation finding
.
In particular, this generalizes and proves Lemma
4.5
.

Lemma 4.14
.

Suppose there exists an algorithm
\mathcal{A}
for
(Restricted)
(\mathcal{H},h)
-separation finding
running in time
f(n,t)
.
Then there is an algorithm that, given an integer
k
and a connected graph
G
of
\mathcal{H}
-treewidth at most
k
,
runs in time
f(n,k)\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
and returns a (restricted)
(\mathcal{H},k,h(k)+1)
-separation decomposition of
G
.
If
\mathcal{A}
runs in polynomial space, then the latter algorithm does as well.

Proof.

We will construct the desired separation decomposition by a recursive process, aided by algorithm
\mathcal{A}
. In the recursion, we have a partial decomposition covering a subset
X
of
V(G)
, and indicate a connected subset
H\subseteq V(G)\setminus X
to be decomposed. Before describing the construction, we show how
\mathcal{A}
can be used to efficiently find an
extremal
separation that covers a given subset
Z
of
H
. The extremal property consists of the fact that the output separation weakly covers a set
Z^{\prime}\supseteq Z
that is itself
(\mathcal{H},k)
-inseparable, or all of
H
.

Claim 4.15
.

In the time and space bounds promised by the lemma we can solve:

Extremal (restricted)
(\mathcal{H},h(k)+1)
-separation finding

Parameter:

k

Input:
A graph
G
of
\mathcal{H}
-treewidth bounded by
k
, an integer
k
, a connected non-empty set
Z\subseteq H\subseteq V(G)
, an
(\mathcal{H},h(k)+1)
-separation
(C,S)
in
G
weakly covering
Z
. In the restricted case, additionally a family
\mathcal{F}
of connected, disjoint,
(\mathcal{H},k)
-inseparable subsets of
V(G)
such that
C
contains at most
k
sets from
\mathcal{F}
.

Task:
Output a triple
(Z^{\prime},C^{\prime},S^{\prime})
such that
(C^{\prime},S^{\prime})
is an
(\mathcal{H},h(k)+1)
-separation in
G
that weakly covers the connected set
Z^{\prime}\supseteq Z
which is either equal to
H
or is
(\mathcal{H},k)
-inseparable and contained in
H
. In the restricted case, ensure that
C^{\prime}
contains at most
k
sets from
\mathcal{F}
.

Proof.

Run
\mathcal{A}
on
G
,
k
, and
Z
(and
\mathcal{F}
, in the restricted case), where we additionally set
t=k
.
If
\mathcal{A}
reports that
Z
is
(\mathcal{H},k)
-inseparable: output
(Z,C,S)
unchanged. Otherwise,
\mathcal{A}
outputs an
(\mathcal{H},h(k))
-separation
(C^{\prime},S^{\prime})
with
Z\subseteq C^{\prime}\cup S^{\prime}
(with
C^{\prime}
containing at most
k
sets from
\mathcal{F}
in the restricted case). Note that
|S^{\prime}|\leq h(k)
while
|S|\leq h(k)+1
. Let
Z^{\prime}
be the connected component of
G[H\cap(C^{\prime}\cup S^{\prime})]
that contains
Z
.
If
Z^{\prime}=H
, then we may simply output
(Z^{\prime},C^{\prime},S^{\prime})
. If
Z^{\prime}\subsetneq H
, then choose an arbitrary vertex
v\in N_{G}(Z^{\prime})\cap H
, which exists since
H
is connected. Recursively solve the problem of covering
Z^{\prime}\cup\{v\}
starting from the separation
(C^{\prime},S^{\prime}\cup\{v\})
(and
\mathcal{F}
, in the restricted case) and output the result. Since the set
Z
strictly grows in each iteration, the recursion depth is at most
n
.
∎

Figure 3
:
Illustration of the proof of Lemma
4.14
: a
call to
Extremal (restricted)
(\mathcal{H},h(k)+1)
-separation finding

with the family
\mathcal{F}_{t}=\{F_{1},F_{2},F_{3}\}
.
An
(\mathcal{H},h(k))
-separation
(C^{\prime},S^{\prime})
weakly covers
Z
.
The set
Z^{\prime}
is the entire green area (both dark and light).
We add vertex
v\in N_{G}(Z^{\prime})\cap H
to
Z^{\prime}
to ensure progress of the process.
The dark green area, i.e.,
Z^{\prime}\cap S^{\prime}
forms a separator between
Z^{\prime}\setminus S^{\prime}
and
H\setminus Z^{\prime}
.
The set
C^{\prime}
contains
F_{2}
but in the restricted setting we require that contains at most
k
such sets.

Using the algorithm for finding extremal separations, we construct the desired separation decomposition recursively. To formalize the subproblem solved by a recursive call, we need the notion of a
partial
(restricted)
(\mathcal{H},k_{1},k_{2})
-separation decomposition of
G
. This is a rooted tree
T
where each node
t\in V(T)
is associated with a triple
(V_{t},C_{t},S_{t})
of subsets of
V(G)
such that:

1.

the subsets
V_{t}
are vertex disjoint, induce non-empty connected subgraphs of
G
, and sum up to a subset
X\subseteq V(G)
which are the vertices
covered
by the partial separation decomposition,

2.

for each
t\in V({T})
the pair
(C_{t},S_{t})
is an
(\mathcal{H},k_{2})
-separation in
G
and
V_{t}\subseteq C_{t}\cup S_{t}
,

3.

each edge
e\in E(G[X])
is either contained inside some
G[V_{t}]
or there exists
t_{1},t_{2}\in V({T})
, such that
t_{1}
is an ancestor of
t_{2}
and
e\in E(V_{t_{1}}\cap S_{t_{1}},V_{t_{2}})
,

4.

if
t
is not a leaf in
T
, then
V_{t}
is
(\mathcal{H},k_{1})
-inseparable,

5.

if
X\neq\emptyset
, then for each connected component
H
of
G-X
, there is a node
t^{*}\in V(T)
such that
N_{G}(H)\subseteq\bigcup_{t\in A_{t^{*}}}(V_{t}\cap S_{t})
, where
A_{t^{*}}
are the ancestors of
t^{*}
in
T
,

6.

additionally, in the case of a restricted partial separation decomposition: for each
t\in V(T)
there are at most
k_{1}
ancestors
s
of
t
such that
V_{s}\subseteq C_{t}
.

Note that if the set
X
of vertices covered by a partial (restricted)
(\mathcal{H},k_{1},k_{2})
-separation decomposition is equal to
V(G)
, then it is a standard (restricted)
(\mathcal{H},k_{1},k_{2})
-separation decomposition following Definitions
4.3
and
4.13
.

Using this notion we can now state the subproblem that we solve recursively to build a (restricted)
(\mathcal{H},k,h(k)+1)
-separation decomposition of
G
. The input is a partial (restricted)
(\mathcal{H},k,h(k)+1)
-separation decomposition covering some set
X\subseteq V(G)
and a connected vertex set
H\subseteq V(G)\setminus X
. The output is a partial (restricted) separation decomposition covering
X\cup H
.

The algorithm solving this subproblem is as follows. Create a new node
t
in the decomposition tree. If
X\neq\emptyset
, then choose
t^{*}\in V(T)
such that
N_{G}(H)\subseteq\bigcup_{t\in A_{t^{*}}}(V_{t}\cap S_{t})
and make
t
a child of
t^{*}
; if
X=\emptyset
then
t
becomes the root of the tree. Let
\mathcal{F}_{t}
denote the family of sets
V_{t_{i}}
for the ancestors
(t_{i})
of node
t
. Let
v
be an arbitrary vertex from
H
. Note that
(C=\emptyset,S=\{v\})
is an
(\mathcal{H},h(k)+1)
-separation in
G
weakly covering
Z:=\{v\}\subseteq H
, so that we can use
(C,S)
as input to
Extremal (restricted)
(\mathcal{H},h)
-separation finding
. In the restricted case, we also give the family
\mathcal{F}_{t}
as input (note that setting
C=\emptyset
meets the precondition for the restricted case). Let
(C^{\prime},S^{\prime})
be the resulting
(\mathcal{H},h(k)+1)
-separation, weakly covering a connected set
Z^{\prime}\subseteq H
which is equal to
H
or
(\mathcal{H},k)
-inseparable. We set
(V_{t},C_{t},S_{t}):=(Z^{\prime},C^{\prime},S^{\prime})
, thereby adding
Z^{\prime}
to the set of vertices covered by the partial decomposition. If
Z^{\prime}=H
then the node
t
becomes a leaf of the decomposition and we are done. If
Z^{\prime}\subsetneq H
, then let
H^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,H^{\prime}_{m}
be the connected components of
G[H\setminus Z^{\prime}]
. One by one we recurse on
H^{\prime}_{i}
to augment the decomposition tree into one that additionally covers
H^{\prime}_{i}
. Based on the guarantees of the subroutine to find extremal separations and the properties of partial separation decompositions, it is straight-forward to verify correctness of the algorithm.

To obtain the desired (restricted)
(\mathcal{H},k,h(k)+1)
-separation decomposition of
G
,
it suffices to solve the subproblem starting from an empty decomposition tree covering the empty set
X
, for
H=V(G)
.
As the overall algorithm performs
n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
calls to
\mathcal{A}
and otherwise consists of operations that run in polynomial time and space, the claimed time and space bounds follow.
∎

4.1.3
Constructing a tree
\mathcal{H}
-decomposition

We proceed analogously as for constructing an
\mathcal{H}
-elimination forest.
We begin with bounding the treewidth of
G\,/\,T
, so we could employ existing algorithms to find a tree decomposition for it.
Then we take advantage of restricted separations to build a tree
\mathcal{H}
-decomposition of
G
by modifying the tree decomposition of
G\,/\,T
.

Lemma 4.16
.

Let
{T}
be an
(\mathcal{H},k_{1},k_{2})
-separation decomposition of a connected graph
G
with
\mathbf{tw}_{\mathcal{H}}(G)\leq k_{1}-1
.
Then the treewidth of
G\,/\,T
is at most
k_{1}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{E}
denote the class of edge-less graphs.
We use notation
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{H}}
to refer to the tree in a tree
\mathcal{H}
-decomposition, in order to distinguish it from the separation decomposition tree
T
.
We are going to transform a tree
\mathcal{H}
-decomposition
(\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{H}},\chi_{\mathcal{H}},L_{\mathcal{H}})
of
G
of width
k_{1}
into
a tree
\mathcal{E}
-decomposition
(\mathcal{T_{E}},\chi_{\mathcal{E}},L_{\mathcal{E}})
of
G\,/\,T
, with
\mathcal{T_{E}}=\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{H}}
.
Recall that
V(G/T)=V(T)
.
For a vertex
v\in V(G)
let
t_{v}\in V(T)
denote the node for which
v\in V_{t_{v}}
.
For
x\in V(\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{E}})
, we define
\chi_{\mathcal{E}}(x)=\{t_{v}\mid v\in\chi_{\mathcal{H}}(x)\}
.
Let
L_{\mathcal{E}}
be the set of these nodes
t\in V(T)
for which
V_{t}\subseteq L_{\mathcal{H}}
.

We need to check that this construction satisfies the properties (
1
)–(
4
) of a tree
\mathcal{E}
-decomposition.
If
t_{1}t_{2}\in E(G\,/\,T)
, then there are
v_{1},v_{2}\in V(G)
such that
v_{1}v_{2}\in E(G),\,v_{1}\in V_{t_{1}},\,v_{2}\in V_{t_{2}}
.
Hence, there must be a bag
\chi_{\mathcal{H}}(x)
containing both
v_{1},v_{2}
, so the bag
\chi_{\mathcal{E}}(x)
contains both
t_{1},t_{2}
.
The bags containing an arbitrary
t\in V(G\,/\,T)
consist of the union of connected subtrees, that is,
\chi_{\mathcal{E}}^{-1}(t)=\{\chi_{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}(v)\mid v\in V_{t}\}
.
Since
V_{t}
is connected, this union is also a connected subtree.
To see property (
3
), consider
t\in V(G\,/\,T)
with
V_{t}\subseteq L_{\mathcal{H}}
.
By Observation
3.7
, a connected subset included in
L_{\mathcal{H}}
must reside in a single bag
\chi_{\mathcal{H}}(x)
.
Therefore
t
belongs only to its counterpart:
\chi_{\mathcal{E}}(x)
.
Finally, by Lemma
4.6
,
L_{\mathcal{E}}
is an independent set in
G\,/\,T
, so
(G\,/\,T)[\chi_{\mathcal{E}}(x)\cap L_{\mathcal{E}}]\in\mathcal{E}
for any
x\in\mathcal{T_{E}}
.
Hence, we have obtained a tree
\mathcal{E}
-decomposition.

For a node
x\in V(\mathcal{T_{E}})
and
t\in\chi_{\mathcal{E}}(x)\setminus L_{\mathcal{E}}
, the set
V_{t}
must include a vertex from
\chi_{\mathcal{H}}(x)\setminus L_{\mathcal{H}}
.
Therefore
|\chi_{\mathcal{E}}(x)\setminus L_{\mathcal{E}}|\leq|\chi_{\mathcal{H}}(x)\setminus L_{\mathcal{H}}|\leq k_{1}
.
This means that the width of
(\mathcal{T_{E}},\chi_{\mathcal{E}},L_{\mathcal{E}})
is at most
k_{1}-1
.
Since the edge-less graphs have treewidth 0, the claim follows from Lemma
3.6
.
∎

The following lemma explains the role of the restricted separation decompositions. Effectively, the additional property of being restricted implies that for each node
t\in V(T)
of an
(\mathcal{H},k_{1},k_{2})
-separation decomposition
T
, we not only know that
C_{t}
induces an
\mathcal{H}
-subgraph with a neighborhood of small size (
k_{2}
), we can also infer that the set
V_{t}\setminus S_{t}=V_{t}\cap C_{t}
induces an
\mathcal{H}
-subgraph with a neighborhood whose size is bounded in terms of
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
.

Lemma 4.17
.

Let
t
be a node in a restricted
(\mathcal{H},k_{1},k_{2})
-separation decomposition
T
of a connected graph
G
and let
A_{t}\subseteq V(T)
be the set of nodes
t_{i}\neq t
such that
E_{G}(V_{t_{i}},V_{t}\setminus S_{t})
is non-empty. The following holds.

(i)

|A_{t}|\leq k_{1}+k_{2}
.

(ii)

N_{G}(V_{t}\setminus S_{t})\subseteq(V_{t}\cap S_{t})\cup\bigcup_{s\in A_{t}}(V_{s}\cap S_{s})
.

(iii)

The pair
(V_{t}\setminus S_{t},N_{G}(V_{t}\setminus S_{t}))
is an
(\mathcal{H},k_{2}(k_{1}+k_{2}+1)
-separation in
G
.

Proof.

(
i
)
By property (
3
) all nodes
t_{i}\in A_{t}
must be ancestors of
t
. Note that
V_{t}\setminus S_{t}\subseteq C_{t}
, so that
N_{G}(V_{t}\setminus S_{t})\subseteq C_{t}\cup S_{t}
since
(C_{t},S_{t})
is an
(\mathcal{H},k_{2})
-separation.

As the decomposition is restricted, there can be at most
k_{1}
many
t_{i}
for which
V_{t_{i}}\subseteq C_{t}
.
Since each set
V_{t_{i}}
induces a connected subgraph of
G
, Observation
4.4
implies that any other
t_{i}
for which
E(V_{t_{i}},V_{t}\setminus S_{t})\neq\emptyset

must satisfy
V_{t_{i}}\cap S_{t}\neq\emptyset
. Since
|S_{t}|\leq k_{2}
and the sets
V_{t_{i}}
are vertex-disjoint,
the claim follows.

(
ii
)
As the sets
V_{s}
in a separation decomposition sum to
V(G)
, each neighbor of
V_{t}\setminus S_{t}
either belongs to
V_{t}\cap S_{t}
or belongs to a set
V_{s}
for
s\neq t
, implying
s\in A_{t}
.

(
iii
)
As
V_{t}\setminus S_{t}\subseteq C_{t}
, we have
G[V_{t}\setminus S_{t}]\in\mathcal{H}
since
G[C_{t}]\in\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{H}
is hereditary. Since (
ii
) shows that
N_{G}(V_{t}\setminus S_{t})
is contained in the union of
1+|A_{t}|\leq 1+k_{1}+k_{2}
sets
S_{s}
, each of which has size at most
k_{2}
, this proves the claim.
∎

The separation guarantee of the preceding lemma allows us to transform tree decompositions of
G/T
for restricted separation decompositions
T
, into tree
\mathcal{H}
-decompositions of
G
.

Lemma 4.18
.

Let
{T}
be a restricted
(\mathcal{H},k_{1},k_{2})
-separation decomposition of
a connected graph
G
.
Suppose we are given a tree decomposition of
G\,/\,T
of width
d-1
.
Then we can construct a tree
\mathcal{H}
-decomposition of
G
of width
d\cdot k_{2}\cdot(k_{1}+k_{2}+1)
in polynomial time.

Proof.

For a node
t\in V(G\,/\,T)
, let
A_{t}
be the set of nodes
t_{i}\neq t
such that
E(V_{t_{i}},V_{t}\setminus S_{t})
is non-empty.
By Lemma
4.17
we have
|A_{t}|\leq k_{1}+k_{2}
.

Given a tree decomposition
(\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{tw}},\chi_{\mathbf{tw}})
of
G\,/\,T
of width
d-1
,
we define
Q=\bigcup_{t\in{V(T)}}(V_{t}\cap S_{t})
.
We reuse the same tree
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{H}}=\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{tw}}
, define
\chi_{\mathcal{H}}(x)=\bigcup_{t\in\chi_{\mathbf{tw}}(x)}\left((V_{t}\cap S_{t})\cup\bigcup_{s\in A_{t}}(V_{s}\cap S_{s})\right)
,
and
L=V(G)\setminus Q
.
In other words, we replace
t
with the “downstairs” separator
V_{t}\cap S_{t}
and “upstairs” separator
\bigcup_{s\in A_{t}}(V_{s}\cap S_{s})
.
Lemma
4.17
ensures that each constructed bag has size at most
\max_{x\in V(\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{tw}})}|\chi_{\mathbf{tw}}(x)|\cdot\max_{t\in V(T)}|S_{t}|\cdot(\max_{t\in V(T)}|A_{t}|+1)\leq d\cdot k_{2}\cdot(k_{1}+k_{2}+1)
.

First, we argue that for any
v\in Q
the set of bags containing
v
forms a connected subtree of
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{H}}
.
For
v\in Q
, let
t_{v}
be the node of
T
with
v\in V_{t}\cap S_{t}
containing
v
.
The vertex
v
appears in the bags which contained
t_{v}
(then
v
is a part of the “downstairs” separator) or some
t
with
t_{v}\in A_{t}
(then
v
is a part of the “upstairs” separator).
Formally, we define
B_{v}=\{t\in V(G\,/\,T)\mid t_{v}\in A_{t}\}
.
Note that
B_{v}\subseteq N_{G\,/\,T}(t_{v})
.
We have
\chi_{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}(v)=\chi_{\mathbf{tw}}^{-1}(t_{v})\cup\bigcup_{t\in B_{v}}\chi_{\mathbf{tw}}^{-1}(t)
.
Hence,
v
appears in bags where either
t_{v}
was located or some of its neighbors from
B_{v}
. This gives a sum of connected subtrees that have non-empty intersections with
\chi_{\mathbf{tw}}^{-1}(t_{v})
.

To finish the construction, we append the connected components of
G[L]=G-Q
, that is, the subgraphs
V_{t}\setminus S_{t}
.
Since
V_{t}\subseteq C_{t}\cup S_{t}
and
C_{t}\in\mathcal{H}
, then also
V_{t}\setminus S_{t}\in\mathcal{H}
.
By Lemma
4.17
, each set
N_{G}(V_{t}\setminus S_{t})
is contained in
(V_{t}\cap S_{t})\cup\bigcup_{s\in A_{t}}(V_{s}\cap S_{s})
, i.e., in the set of vertices that was put in place of
t
.
We can thus choose any node
x\in\chi_{\mathbf{tw}}^{-1}(t)
, make a new node
x_{t}
, connect
x_{t}
to
x
, and set
\chi_{\mathcal{H}}(x_{t})=\chi_{\mathcal{H}}(x)\cup(V_{t}\setminus S_{t})
.
This preserves the property that sets
\chi_{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}(v)
are connected.

Finally, we argue that for any edge
uv\in E(G)
, there is a node
x
such that
\{u,v\}\subseteq\chi_{\mathcal{H}}(x)
.
If
u\in V_{t}\setminus S_{t}
for some
t
, then
either
uv\in E(G[V_{t}])
or
uv\in E(V_{s}\cap S_{s},V_{t}\setminus S_{t})
for some
s\in A_{t}

(we cannot have any edges in
E(V_{s}\setminus S_{s},V_{t}\setminus S_{t})
since one of these nodes would be an ancestor of the other one and this would contradict property (
3
) of Definition
4.3
).
Then both
u,v
are contained in
\chi_{\mathcal{H}}(x_{t})
.
The case
v\in V_{t}\setminus S_{t}
is symmetric, so the remaining case is when
u,v\in Q
.
Again, if
uv\in E(G[V_{t}])
for some
t
, then
u,v
are contained in
\chi_{\mathcal{H}}(x_{t})
.
Otherwise,
uv\in E(V_{t_{1}}\cap S_{t_{1}},V_{t_{2}}\cap S_{t_{2}})
for
t_{1}t_{2}\in E(G\,/\,T)
.
Then there exists
x
for which
t_{1},t_{2}\in\chi_{\mathbf{tw}}(x)
and thus
u,v\in\chi_{\mathcal{H}}(x)
.
∎

Lemma 4.19
.

Suppose there exists an algorithm
\mathcal{A}
for
Restricted
(\mathcal{H},h)
-separation finding
running in time
f(n,t)
.
Then there is an algorithm that,
given graph
G
with
\mathcal{H}
-treewidth
k-1
,
runs in time
\left(f(n,k)+2^{\mathcal{O}(k)}\right)\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
, and returns a tree
\mathcal{H}
-decomposition of
G
of width
\mathcal{O}((h(k))^{2}\cdot k)
.

Proof.

It suffices to process each connected component of
G
independently, so we can assume that
G
is connected.
We use Lemma
4.5
to find a restricted
(\mathcal{H},k,h(k)+1)
-separation decomposition
{T}
in time
f(n,k)\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.
The graph
G\,/\,T
is guaranteed by Lemma
4.16
to have treewidth at most
k
.
We find a tree decomposition of
G\,/\,T
of width
\mathcal{O}(k)
in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(k)}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}

[
14
,
32
]
. We turn it into a tree
\mathcal{H}
-decomposition of
G
of width
h(k)\cdot(k+h(k)+1)\cdot\mathcal{O}(k)=\mathcal{O}((h(k))^{2}\cdot k)
in polynomial time with Lemma
4.18
.
∎

Similarly to the construction of an
\mathcal{H}
-elimination forest,
we can replace the approximation algorithm for computing the tree decomposition of
G\,/\,T
with one that works in polynomial space.
For this purpose, we could use the known
\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\log\mathbf{tw}})
-approximation which runs in polynomial time (and space)
[
41
, Thm 6.4]
.
This construction is not particularly interesting though, because all the considered algorithms exploiting tree
\mathcal{H}
-decompositions require exponential space.

4.2
Finding separations

We have established a framework that allows us to construct
\mathcal{H}
-elimination forests and tree
\mathcal{H}
-decompositions for any hereditary class
\mathcal{H}
, as long as we can supply an efficient parameterized algorithm for
(\mathcal{H},h)
-separation finding
.
In this section we provide such algorithms for various graph classes.
Their running times and the approximation guarantee
h
vary over different classes and they govern the efficiency of the decomposition finding procedures.

A crucial tool employed in several arguments is the theory of important separators (Definition
3.1
).
We begin with a few observations about them.
For two disjoint sets
X,Y\subseteq V(G)
, let
\mathcal{S}(X,Y)
be the set of all important
(X,Y)
-separators and
\mathcal{S}_{k}(X,Y)
be the subset of
\mathcal{S}(X,Y)
consisting of separators of size at most
k
. We say that an algorithm
enumerates the set
\mathcal{S}_{k}(X,Y)
in polynomial space
if it runs in polynomial space and outputs a member of
\mathcal{S}_{k}(X,Y)
at several steps during its computation, so that each member of
\mathcal{S}_{k}
is outputted exactly once.

Theorem 4.20

(
[
32
, Thm. 8.51]
)
.

For any disjoint
X,Y\subseteq V(G)
the set

\mathcal{S}_{k}(X,Y)
can be enumerated in time
\mathcal{O}^{*}(|\mathcal{S}_{k}(X,Y)|)
and polynomial space.

Note that a polynomial-space algorithm cannot store all relevant important separators to output the entire set
\mathcal{S}_{k}(X,Y)
at the end, since the cardinality of
\mathcal{S}_{k}(X,Y)
can be exponential in
k
. While the original statement of
4.20
does not mention the polynomial bound on the space usage of the algorithm, it is not difficult to see that the algorithm indeed uses polynomial space. At its core, the enumeration algorithm for important separators is a bounded-depth search tree algorithm. Each step of the algorithm uses a maximum-flow computation to find a minimum
(X,Y)
-separator which is as far from
X
as possible, and then branches in two directions by selecting a suitable vertex
v\notin X\cup Y
and either adding
v
to the separator or adding it to
X
. In the base case of the recursion, the algorithm outputs an important separator.

Lemma 4.21

(
[
32
, Lemma 8.52]
)
.

For any disjoint
X,Y\subseteq V(G)
it holds that
\sum_{S\in\mathcal{S}(X,Y)}4^{-|S|}\leq 1
.

In particular this implies that
|\mathcal{S}_{k}(X,Y)|\leq 4^{k}
.
The next observation makes a connection between important separators and
(\mathcal{H},k)
-separations, which will allow us to perform branching according to the choice of an important separator.

Lemma 4.22
.

Let
Z\subseteq V(G)
be a connected set of vertices. Suppose there is an
(\mathcal{H},k)
-separation
(C,S)
that covers
Z
and a vertex set
F
such that
S
is an
(F,Z)
-separator.
Then there exists an
(\mathcal{H},k)
-separation
(C^{*},S^{*})
covering
Z
such that
S^{*}
contains an important
(F,Z)
-separator
S^{\prime}
.
Furthermore,

(C^{*},S^{*}\setminus S^{\prime})
is an
(\mathcal{H},\,k-|S^{\prime}|)
-separation covering
Z
in
G-S^{\prime}
and if
(\widehat{C},\widehat{S})
is an
(\mathcal{H},\,k^{\prime})
-separation covering
Z
in
G-S^{\prime}
,
then
(\widehat{C},\widehat{S}\cup S^{\prime})
is an
(\mathcal{H},\,k^{\prime}+|S^{\prime}|)
-separation covering
Z
in
G
.

Proof.

We first show the existence of
(C^{*},S^{*})
.
Let
(C,S)
be an
(\mathcal{H},k)
-separation covering
Z
such that
S
is an
(F,Z)
-separator. This exists by the premise of the lemma. If
S
contains an important
(F,Z)
-separator, then with
(C^{*},S^{*})=(C,S)
the first claim holds.

So suppose that this is not the case.
Clearly
N_{G}(R_{S}(F))\subseteq S
is an
(F,Z)
-separator, so there exists an inclusion-minimal
(F,Z)
-separator
S_{F}\subseteq N_{G}(R_{S}(F))
. As
S_{F}\subseteq S
is not important by assumption, by Lemma
3.2
there exists an important
(F,Z)
-separator
S_{F}^{\prime}
satisfying
R_{S_{F}}(F)\subsetneq R_{S_{F}^{\prime}}(F)
and
|S_{F}^{\prime}|\leq|S_{F}|
.
We show that
(C^{*}=R_{S^{*}}(Z),S^{*}=(S\setminus S_{F})\cup S_{F}^{\prime})
is an
(\mathcal{H},k)
-separation. Clearly
|S^{*}|\leq|S|\leq k
and
(C^{*},S^{*})
covers
Z
. Since
C^{*}
is the set of reachable vertices from
Z
in
G-S^{*}
, it follows that
G[C^{*}]
is a connected component of
G-S^{*}
. We show that
C^{*}\subseteq C
. Suppose not. Since
Z
is connected, so is
G[C^{*}]
. Consider a path
P
in
G[C^{*}]
from
Z
to a vertex in
C^{*}\setminus C
. Since
G[C]
also contains
Z
, the first vertex on
P
that is not in
C
, is a vertex
v\in N_{G}(C)\cap(C^{*}\setminus C)
. Then
v\notin(S\setminus S_{F})
, since
S\setminus S_{F}\subseteq S^{*}
. As
N_{G}(C)\subseteq S
, we therefore have
v\in S_{F}
. Since
S_{F}\ni v
is an
inclusion-minimal

(F,Z)
-separator in
G
, there exists
u\in N_{G}(v)\cap R_{S_{F}}(F)
. But this leads to a contradiction: as
R_{S^{\prime}_{F}}(F)\supsetneq R_{S_{F}}(F)
there is a path from
F
to
u
in
G-S^{\prime}_{F}
, while
v\in C^{*}
yields a path from
v
to
Z
in
G-S^{\prime}_{F}
as
S^{\prime}_{F}\subseteq S^{*}
, so the combination of these two paths with the edge
uv
yields a path from
F
to
Z
in
G-S^{\prime}_{F}
; a contradiction to the assumption that
S^{\prime}_{F}
is an
(F,Z)
-separator. Hence
C^{*}\subseteq C
, and since
\mathcal{H}
is hereditary we have
G[C^{*}]\in\mathcal{H}
.

Next, let
S^{\prime}\subseteq S^{*}
and
(C^{*},S^{*})
be an
(\mathcal{H},k)
-separation covering
Z
in
G
.
Since
N_{G}(C^{*})\subseteq S^{*}
, we have
N_{G-S^{\prime}}(C^{*})\subseteq S^{*}\setminus S^{\prime}
, and
therefore
(C^{*},S^{*}\setminus S^{\prime})
is an
(\mathcal{H},\,k-|S^{\prime}|)
-separation covering
Z
in
G-S^{\prime}
.

Analogously, we argue that if
(\widehat{C},\widehat{S})
is an
(\mathcal{H},\,k^{\prime})
-separation covering
Z
in
G-S^{\prime}
, then
(\widehat{C},\widehat{S}\cup S^{\prime})
is an
(\mathcal{H},\,k^{\prime}+|S^{\prime}|)
-separation covering
Z
in
G
. We have
G[\widehat{C}]\in\mathcal{H}
and
|\widehat{S}\cup S^{\prime}|=k^{\prime}+|S^{\prime}|
. Since
N_{G}(\widehat{C})\subseteq N_{G-S^{\prime}}(\widehat{C})\cup S^{\prime}\subseteq\widehat{S}\cup S^{\prime}
, the pair
(\widehat{C},\widehat{S}\cup S^{\prime})
is indeed an
(\mathcal{H},k^{\prime}+|S^{\prime}|)
separation in
G
, which clearly covers
Z
.
∎

4.2.1
Classes excluding induced subgraphs

Within this section, let
\mathcal{H}
be a class given by a finite family
\mathcal{F}
of forbidden induced subgraphs, so that
G\in\mathcal{H}
if and only if
G
does not contain any graph from
\mathcal{F}
as an induced subgraph.
Note that such a formulation allows us to express also classes excluding non-induced subgraphs.
Unlike further examples, in this setting we do not need to settle for approximation and we present an exact algorithm for separation finding.
The algorithm below solves
the
(\mathcal{H},h)
-separation finding
problem for
h(x)=x
with a single-exponential dependence on the parameter.
Note that
it does not rely on the assumption of bounded
\mathcal{H}
-treewidth.

Lemma 4.23
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a class given by a finite family
\mathcal{F}
of forbidden induced subgraphs.
There is an algorithm that, given an
n
-vertex graph
G
, an integer
k
, and a connected non-empty set
Z\subseteq V(G)
,
runs in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(k)}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
and polynomial space, and returns an
(\mathcal{H},k)
-separation covering
Z
or concludes that
Z
is
(\mathcal{H},k)
-inseparable.

Proof.

Let
(G,Z,k)
be the input. We present a recursive algorithm that works under the assumption that
there exists an
(\mathcal{H},k)
-separation
(C,S)
covering

Z
and outputs an
(\mathcal{H},k)
-separation covering
Z
.
If the algorithm fails to output a separation during its execution, we shall conclude that
Z
is
(\mathcal{H},k)
-inseparable. Since it suffices to consider the connected component containing
Z
, we may assume that
G
is connected.

Let
q
denote the maximal number of vertices among graphs in
\mathcal{F}
.
We can check in time
\mathcal{O}(n^{q})
whether
G
contains an induced subgraph
F
isomorphic to one from
\mathcal{F}
.
If not, we can simply return
(V(G),\emptyset)
.

It cannot be that
V(F)\subseteq C
, so there exists a vertex
u\in V(F)\setminus Z
such that either
u\in S
or
S
is a
(u,Z)
-separator.
In the first case
(C,S\setminus u)
is an answer for the instance
(G-u,Z,k-1)
,
which we solve recursively.
In the second case, by Lemma
4.22
, there exists an important
(u,Z)
-separator
S^{\prime}
, such that
|S^{\prime}|\leq k
and there exists an
(\mathcal{H},k)
-separation
(C^{*},S^{*})
covering
Z
with
S^{\prime}\subseteq S^{*}
.
Note that since we have assumed that
G
is connected, the separator
S^{\prime}
is always non-empty.
Then
(C^{*},S^{*}\setminus S^{\prime})
is an answer for the instance
(G-S^{\prime},Z,k-|S^{\prime}|)
.
We enumerate all separators from
\mathcal{S}_{k}(u,Z)
and
branch on all these possibilities, over all (at most
q
) ways to choose
u
. If any recursive call returns a separation, then we can complete it to an answer for
(G,Z,k)
.

We shall bound the maximal number of leaves
T(k)
in the recursion tree with respect to parameter
k
.
For each
u\in V(F)\setminus Z

the first branching rule leads to at most
T(k-1)
leaves and
the second one gives
\sum_{S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{k}(u,Z)}T(k-|S^{\prime}|)
.
Let us show inductively that
T(k)\leq(5q)^{k}
, first upper bounding the summand coming from the important separators for one choice of
u
.

\displaystyle\sum_{S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{k}(u,Z)}T(k-|S^{\prime}|)\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{k}(u,Z)}(5q)^{k-|S^{\prime}|}\leq\sum_{S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{k}(u,Z)}\left(\frac{5q}{4}\right)^{k-|S^{\prime}|}4^{k}\cdot 4^{-|S^{\prime}|}

\displaystyle\leq\,

\displaystyle\left(\frac{5q}{4}\right)^{k-1}4^{k}\sum_{S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{k}(u,Z)}4^{-|S^{\prime}|}\leq 4\cdot(5q)^{k-1}\quad\text{(Lemma~\ref{lem:important-sum})}

\displaystyle T(k)\leq\sum_{u\in V(F)\setminus Z}\left(T(k-1)+\sum_{S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{k}(u,Z)}T(k-|S^{\prime}|)\right)\leq q\cdot\left((5q)^{k-1}+4\cdot(5q)^{k-1}\right)=(5q)^{k}

Since the depth of the recursion tree is
k
, we obtain a bound of
k\cdot(5q)^{k}
on the number of recursive calls.
By Theorem
4.20
, the time spent in each call is proportional to the number of children in the recursion tree times a polynomial factor. As the sum of the numbers of children, taken over all the nodes in the recursion tree, is bounded by its total size
k\cdot T(k)
, this proves the lemma.
∎

4.2.2
Bipartite graphs

Let
\mathsf{bip}
denote the class of bipartite graphs.
For
\mathcal{H}=\mathsf{bip}
we present a polynomial-time algorithm, which gives a 2-approximation to the task of separation finding.
Therefore, we obtain a better running time but worse approximation guarantee than in the previous section.
Again, we do not rely on the bounded
\mathsf{bip}
-treewidth. The 2-approximation for separation finding for bipartite graphs is related to the half-integrality of 0/1/
all
constraint satisfaction problems as studied by Iwata, Yamaguchi, and Yoshida
[
65
]
. To avoid the notational overhead of their framework, we give an elementary algorithm below.

The presented algorithm works on a parity graph
G^{\prime}
:
each vertex
v
from the original graph
G
is replaced by two parity-copies
v^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime}
and each edge in
G^{\prime}
switches the parity of a vertex.
A path in
G
is odd if and only if the endpoints of its counterpart in
G^{\prime}
have different parity.
Hence, we can express the task of hitting odd cycles in terms of finding a separator in the parity graph.

For
A\subseteq V(G)
, we say we identify
A
into a vertex
v\notin V(G)
, if we add vertex
v
to
G
with
N_{G}(v)=\bigcup_{u\in A}N_{G}(u)\setminus A
, followed by deleting
A
.

Lemma 4.24
.

There is a polynomial-time algorithm for
(
\mathsf{bip}
,
h(x)=2x
)-separation finding
.

Proof.

Let
G
be a graph,
k
be an integer, and
Z\subseteq V(G)
be non-empty such that
G[Z]
is connected.
If
k=0
, then let
C
be the connected component of
G
that contains
Z
. The separation
(C,\emptyset)
is a (
\mathsf{bip}
,
0
)-separation if
G[C]
is bipartite, otherwise
Z
is (
\mathsf{bip}
,
0
)-inseparable. Similarly, if
G[Z]
is not bipartite then we simply conclude that
Z
is (
\mathsf{bip}
,
2k
)-inseparable. In the remainder, assume that
G[Z]
is bipartite and
k>0
.
Compute a proper 2-coloring
c\colon Z\to[2]
of
G[Z]
, which is unique up to relabeling since
G[Z]
is connected. Let
G_{Z}
be the graph obtained from
G
by identifying each color class into a single vertex. That is, we identify
c^{-1}(1)
into a vertex
v_{1}
and
c^{-1}(2)
into a vertex
v_{2}
. If
Z
consists of a single vertex, then without loss of generality assume only
v_{1}
exists, the rest of the construction remains the same up to ignoring
v_{2}
.
Next we construct a graph
G_{Z}^{\prime}
that contains a false twin for every vertex in
G_{Z}
in order to keep track of parity of paths. More formally, let
V(G_{Z}^{\prime})=\{u^{\prime},u^{\prime\prime}\mid u\in G_{Z}\}
and
E(G_{Z}^{\prime})=\{x^{\prime}y^{\prime\prime}\mid{xy}\in E(G_{Z})\}
. Intuitively, an odd cycle in
G_{Z}
that contains some vertex
u
corresponds to a path in
G^{\prime}_{Z}
from
u^{\prime}
to its false-twin copy
u^{\prime\prime}
. In what follows we show that breaking such paths for a good choice of endpoints results in the desired
(\mathsf{bip}
,
2k
)-separation.

Claim 4.25
.

If the set
S\subseteq V(G_{Z}^{\prime})
is a
(\{v_{1}^{\prime},v_{2}^{\prime\prime}\},\{v_{1}^{\prime\prime},v_{2}^{\prime}\})
-separator in
G_{Z}^{\prime}
, then the connected component of
G-\{u\mid u^{\prime}\in S\vee u^{\prime\prime}\in S\}
that contains
Z
is bipartite.

Proof.

Suppose
S\subseteq V(G_{Z}^{\prime})
is a
(\{v_{1}^{\prime},v_{2}^{\prime\prime}\},\{v_{1}^{\prime\prime},v_{2}^{\prime}\})
-separator in
G_{Z}^{\prime}
. Let
X=\{u\mid u^{\prime}\in S\vee u^{\prime\prime}\in S\}
. For the sake of contradiction, suppose that the component of
Z
in
G-X
is not bipartite. Let
Q
be an odd cycle in said component, and let
Q_{Z}
be the walk in
G_{Z}-X
obtained by replacing each
u\in Z\cap Q
by the vertex
v_{c(u)}
into which it was identified.
Clearly
Q_{Z}
is a closed odd walk in
G_{Z}-X
, since there are no two consecutive vertices of
Q
in
Z\cap Q
that are in the same color class.
As
Z
is connected, we have
v_{1}v_{2}\in E(G_{Z})
, so
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
lie in a single connected component of
G_{Z}-X
.
We obtain that this component contains an odd cycle. Let
O=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{2k+1})
be a shortest odd cycle in
G_{Z}-X
in the component of
\{v_{1},v_{2}\}
. Let
X^{\prime}=\{u^{\prime},u^{\prime\prime}\mid u\in X\}
; note that
S\subseteq X^{\prime}
. We do a case distinction on
|V(O)\cap\{v_{1},v_{2}\}|
, in each case we show there exists a path in
G_{Z}^{\prime}-S
that contradicts that
S
is a
(\{v_{1}^{\prime},v_{2}^{\prime\prime}\},\{v_{1}^{\prime\prime},v_{2}^{\prime}\})
-separator.

If
|V(O)\cap\{v_{1},v_{2}\}|=0
, then since
O
and
\{v_{1},v_{2}\}
are in the same connected component of
G_{Z}-X
, there is a path
P
in
G_{Z}^{\prime}-X^{\prime}
from
v_{1}^{\prime}
to either
u_{1}^{\prime}
or
u_{1}^{\prime\prime}
depending on the parity of
|P|
. Suppose
P
ends in
u_{1}^{\prime}
(the argument for
P
ending in
u_{1}^{\prime\prime}
is symmetric), that is,
P=(v_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,u_{1}^{\prime})
. Then
P
concatenated with
(u_{2}^{\prime\prime},u_{3}^{\prime},\ldots,u_{2k+1}^{\prime},u_{1}^{\prime\prime})
ends at
u_{1}^{\prime\prime}
in
G_{Z}^{\prime}-X^{\prime}
, since
O
is an odd cycle. But then taking the twins of
P
in the reverse direction, we arrive at
v_{1}^{\prime\prime}
. Since
G_{Z}^{\prime}-X^{\prime}

is an induced subgraph of
G_{Z}^{\prime}-S
, it follows that
S
does not contain a single vertex of this path, and hence
S
cannot be a
(\{v_{1}^{\prime},v_{2}^{\prime\prime}\},\{v_{1}^{\prime\prime},v_{2}^{\prime}\})
-separator.

If
|V(O)\cap\{v_{1},v_{2}\}|=1
, then by re-numbering the cycle
O
if needed, we may assume
V(O)\cap\{v_{1},v_{2}\}=\{u_{1}\}
. Assume
u_{1}=v_{1}
; the case
u_{1}=v_{2}
is symmetric. Then
P=(v_{1}^{\prime},u_{2}^{\prime\prime},u_{3}^{\prime},\ldots,\allowbreak u_{2k+1}^{\prime},v_{1}^{\prime\prime})
is a
v_{1}^{\prime}v_{1}^{\prime\prime}
-path in
G_{Z}^{\prime}-S
. It follows that
S
cannot be a
(\{v_{1}^{\prime},v_{2}^{\prime\prime}\},\{v_{1}^{\prime\prime},v_{2}^{\prime}\})
-separator.

Finally, consider the case
|V(O)\cap\{v_{1},v_{2}\}|=2
. First note that
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
are consecutive in
O
. To see this, for any odd cycle that contains both
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
, exactly one of the
v_{1}v_{2}
-path or the
v_{2}v_{1}
-path contains an even number of edges, which can be combined with the edge
v_{1}v_{2}
to form an odd cycle. Since
O
is a shortest odd cycle,
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
are consecutive in
O
. Without loss of generality assume
u_{1}=v_{1}
and
u_{2k+1}=v_{2}
. Then
(v_{1}^{\prime}
,
u_{2}^{\prime\prime}
,…,
v_{2}^{\prime})
is a path of even length in
G_{Z}^{\prime}-S
. It follows that
S
cannot be a
(\{v_{1}^{\prime},v_{2}^{\prime\prime}\},\{v_{1}^{\prime\prime},v_{2}^{\prime}\})
-separator.
∎

Claim 4.26
.

If
Z
is (
\mathsf{bip}
,
k
)-separable, then
G_{Z}^{\prime}
has a
(\{v_{1}^{\prime},v_{2}^{\prime\prime}\},\{v_{1}^{\prime\prime},v_{2}^{\prime}\})
-separator of size at most
2k
.

Proof.

Suppose
Z
is (
\mathsf{bip}
,
k
)-separable and let
(C,X)
be a (
\mathsf{bip}
,
k
)-separation that covers
Z
. We show that
X^{\prime}=\{u^{\prime},u^{\prime\prime}\mid u\in X\}
is a
(\{v_{1}^{\prime},v_{2}^{\prime\prime}\},\{v_{1}^{\prime\prime},v_{2}^{\prime}\})
-separator in
G_{Z}^{\prime}
. Clearly
|X^{\prime}|\leq 2k
. Note that
X^{\prime}\subseteq V(G_{Z}^{\prime})\setminus\{v_{1}^{\prime},v_{1}^{\prime\prime},v_{2}^{\prime},v_{2}^{\prime\prime}\}
since
Z\subseteq C
.
Suppose
X^{\prime}
is not a
(\{v_{1}^{\prime},v_{2}^{\prime\prime}\},\{v_{1}^{\prime\prime},v_{2}^{\prime}\})
-separator in
G_{Z}^{\prime}
. We consider the cases that there is a
v_{1}^{\prime}v_{1}^{\prime\prime}
-path and a
v_{1}^{\prime}v_{2}^{\prime}
-path; the other cases are symmetric.

Let
P^{\prime}
be a
v_{1}^{\prime}v_{1}^{\prime\prime}
-path in
G_{Z}^{\prime}-X^{\prime}
, then
P^{\prime}
has an odd number of edges. The walk in
G_{Z}
obtained from
P^{\prime}
by dropping all parity information is also odd and has empty intersection with
X
because the set
X^{\prime}
is symmetric. Hence,
G_{Z}-X
contains an odd cycle
P
.

•

If
P
contains the edge
v_{1}v_{2}
, then the remainder of
P
is a path of even length connecting
v_{1}
to
v_{2}
. As
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
were obtained by identifying the color classes of
Z
, it follows that there is an even-length walk
P^{*}
in
G-X
from a vertex
z_{1}\in Z\cap c^{-1}(1)
to a vertex
z_{2}\in Z\cap c^{-1}(2)
. As
G[Z]
is bipartite and connected, with
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
of opposite colors, we can extend this even walk
P^{*}
with an odd-length path from
z_{1}
to
z_{2}
in
G[Z]-X
, to obtain an odd closed walk in the connected component of
G-X
containing
Z
; a contradiction to the assumption that
(C,X)
is a (
\mathsf{bip}
,
k
)-separation that covers
Z
.

•

If
P
does not contain the edge
v_{1}v_{2}
, then we can lift
P
to a closed odd walk in the component of
G-X
that contains
Z
, as follows. For every occurrence of some
v_{j}\in\{v_{1},v_{2}\}
on
P
, let
p
and
q
be the predecessor and successor of
v_{j}
on
P
. By the assumption of this case,
p,q\notin\{v_{1},v_{2}\}
. By construction of
G_{Z}
, the fact that
p,q\in N_{G_{Z}}(v_{j})
implies that there exist
z_{p}\in c^{-1}(j)\cap Z\cap N_{G}(p)
and
z_{q}\in c^{-1}(j)\cap Z\cap N_{G}(q)
. Since
G[Z]
is connected, there is a path
P_{p,q}
from
z_{p}
to
z_{q}
in
G[Z]
, and as
G[Z]
is bipartite and
z_{p},z_{q}
belong to the same color class, this path has an even number of edges. Hence we can replace the occurrence of
v_{j}
on walk
P
by the path
P_{p,q}
, which increases the number of edges on the walk by an even number and therefore preserves its parity. Replacing each occurrence of
v_{j}\in\{v_{1},v_{2}\}
on
P
by a path
P_{p,q}
in this way, we obtain a closed walk
P^{*}
of odd length in the connected component
G-X
that contains
Z
; a contradiction.

Finally let
P^{\prime}
be a
v_{1}^{\prime}v_{2}^{\prime}
-path in
G_{Z}^{\prime}-X^{\prime}
, then
P^{\prime}
has an even number of edges. Let
P
be the walk in
G_{Z}-X
obtained from
P^{\prime}
by dropping all parity information,
P
is an even walk in
G_{Z}-X
in the component of
\{v_{1},v_{2}\}
. Similarly as in the first bullet above, turn
P
into a closed odd walk by making the last step from
v_{2}
to
v_{1}
with an odd-length path through
G[Z]
. Again we reach a contradiction for the fact that
(C,X)
was a (
\mathsf{bip}
,
k
)-separation that covers
Z
.
∎

Now we can find the desired separation covering
Z
by first constructing
G_{Z}^{\prime}
and then computing a
(\{v_{1}^{\prime},v_{2}^{\prime\prime}\},\{v_{1}^{\prime\prime},v_{2}^{\prime}\})
-separator, for example using the Ford-Fulkerson algorithm. If the latter is larger than
2k
, return that
Z
is (
\mathsf{bip}
,
2k
)-inseparable. Otherwise, return the separation given by the first claim together with the connected component of
Z
. Clearly these operations can be performed in polynomial time.
∎

4.2.3
Separation via packing-covering duality

In this section we present the most general way of finding separations, which is based on a packing-covering duality.
We show that the existence of an efficient parameterized (or approximation) algorithm for
\mathcal{H}
-deletion
entails the existence of an efficient algorithm for
(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{O}(k^{2}))
-separation finding
.

The relation between the maximum size of a packing of obstructions to
\mathcal{H}
(e.g., odd cycles for the case
\mathcal{H}=\mathsf{bip}
) and the minimum size of an
\mathcal{H}
-deletion set is
called the Erdős-Pósa property for obstructions to
\mathcal{H}
, after the famous result on the packing-covering duality for cycles
[
40
]
.
It is known that graphs with bounded treewidth enjoy such a packing-covering duality
for connected obstructions for various graph classes.
We show that this relation also holds for graphs with bounded
\mathcal{H}
-treewidth and obstructions to
\mathcal{H}
.
This is the only place where the assumption of bounded
\mathcal{H}
-treewidth plays a role for constructing algorithms for
(\mathcal{H},h)
-separation finding
.

Lemma 4.27
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a hereditary union-closed graph class and
G
be a graph satisfying

\mathbf{tw}_{\mathcal{H}}(G)\leq k
.
Then
G
either contains
k+1
disjoint vertex sets
V_{1},\dots,V_{k+1}\subseteq V(G)
such that
G[V_{i}]\not\in\mathcal{H}
and
G[V_{i}]
is connected for all
i\in[k+1]
, or an
\mathcal{H}
-deletion set of size at most
k(k+1)
.

Proof.

Consider a tree
\mathcal{H}
-decomposition
(\mathcal{T},\chi,L)
of
G
of width at most
k
and root it at an arbitrary node
r
.
For
t\in V(\mathcal{T})
let
S_{t}=\chi(t)\setminus L
and let
V_{t}
denote the set of vertices occurring in bags in the subtree of
\mathcal{T}
rooted at
t
(including
\chi(t)
).

If
G\in\mathcal{H}{}
, then the empty set is an
\mathcal{H}
-deletion set of size
0
and the lemma holds. If not, consider a node
t
for which
G[V_{t}]\not\in\mathcal{H}
and
t
is furthest from the root among all such nodes.
By Observation
3.7
we have that
N(\chi(t)\cap L)\subseteq S_{t}
and by definition
G[\chi(t)\cap L]\in\mathcal{H}
.
Also
G[V_{t_{i}}]\in\mathcal{H}
for any child
t_{i}
of
t
, by the choice of
t
, and thus
G[V_{t_{i}}\setminus S_{t}]\in\mathcal{H}
.
Since
\mathcal{H}
is closed under disjoint unions, we get that the union of all these subgraphs, i.e.,
G[V_{t}\setminus S_{t}]
also belongs to
\mathcal{H}
.
Hence, removing
S_{t}
separates
G
into
G-V_{t}
and a subgraph from
\mathcal{H}
.

We define
S_{1}=S_{t},\,G_{1}=G[V_{t}]
, and iterate this procedure on
G^{\prime}=G-V_{t}
.
If after
\ell\leq k
steps we have reached a graph that belongs to
\mathcal{H}
,
then
S=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}S_{i}
is an
\mathcal{H}
-deletion set and
|S|\leq\ell\cdot\max_{i=1}^{\ell}|S_{i}|\leq k(k+1)
.
Otherwise the induced subgraphs
G_{1},G_{2},\dots,G_{k+1}
are vertex-disjoint and
G_{i}\not\in\mathcal{H}
for each
i\in[k+1]
.
Since
\mathcal{H}
is closed under disjoint unions, for each
i\in[k+1]
there is a
subset
V^{\prime}_{i}\subseteq V(G_{i})
such that
G[V^{\prime}_{i}]
is
connected and does not belong to
\mathcal{H}
.
∎

So far we have given an existential proof that under the assumption of bounded
\mathcal{H}
-treewidth the obstructions to
\mathcal{H}
enjoy a packing-covering duality.
Next, we show that when a graph admits a packing of
k+1
obstructions to
\mathcal{H}
and set
Z
is
(\mathcal{H},k)
-separable, then we can detect one obstruction that can cheaply be separated from
Z
.
Let
\mathbf{dn}_{\mathcal{H}}{}(G)
(for
\mathcal{H}
-deletion number
) denote the minimum size of an
\mathcal{H}
-deletion set in
G
.

Lemma 4.28
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a hereditary union-closed graph class such that there exists an algorithm
\mathcal{A}
that tests membership of an
n
-vertex graph in
\mathcal{H}
in time
f(n)
.
Then there is an algorithm that, given integers
k\leq t
, a graph
G
satisfying

\mathbf{tw}_{\mathcal{H}}(G)\leq t
, and an
(\mathcal{H},k)
-separable set
Z\subseteq V(G)
, runs in time
4^{k}\cdot(f(n)+n^{\mathcal{O}(1)})
and polynomial space; it either correctly concludes that
\mathbf{dn}_{\mathcal{H}}{}(G)\leq t(t+1)
or outputs a set
F\subseteq V(G)
such that (1)
G[F]\not\in\mathcal{H}
, (2)
G[F]
is connected, (3)
N_{G}[F]\cap Z=\emptyset
, and (4)
|N_{G}(F)|\leq k
.
If
\mathcal{A}
runs in polynomial space, then the latter algorithm does as well.

Proof.

By
Lemma

4.27
either
\mathbf{dn}_{\mathcal{H}}{}(G)\leq t(t+1)
or there exist
t+1
disjoint vertex sets
V_{1},\dots,V_{t+1}
such that
G[V_{i}]\not\in\mathcal{H}
and
G[V_{i}]
is connected.
We present an algorithm that outputs the requested set
F
under the assumption that the second scenario holds.
If the algorithm fails to output
F
, we shall conclude that
\mathbf{dn}_{\mathcal{H}}{}(G)\leq t(t+1)
.

We first present the algorithm and then argue for its correctness.
For each vertex
v\in V(G)\setminus N_{G}[Z]

we enumerate all important
(v,Z)
-separators of size at most
k
.
There are at most
4^{k}
such separators (
Lemma

4.21
) for a fixed
v
and they can be enumerated in time
\mathcal{O}^{*}(4^{k})
(
4.20
).
For a separator
S^{\prime}
we consider the set
F_{v}^{S^{\prime}}=R_{S^{\prime}}(\{v\})
(recall Definition
3.1
). We have
N_{G}(F_{v}^{S^{\prime}})\subseteq S^{\prime}
(since the separator
S^{\prime}
is important, it is also inclusion-wise minimal, so the equality holds as well) and therefore
F_{v}^{S^{\prime}}
satisfies conditions (2)–(4).
We check whether
G[F_{v}^{S^{\prime}}]\in\mathcal{H}
, in time
f(n)
: if not, we simply output
F=F_{v}^{S^{\prime}}
.

Now we analyze the algorithm.
Let
(C,S)
be an
(\mathcal{H},k)
-separation covering
Z
, which exists by assumption.
We have also assumed the existence of
t+1
disjoint vertex sets
V_{1},\dots,V_{t+1}
such that
G[V_{i}]\not\in\mathcal{H}
and
G[V_{i}]
is connected.
Since
k\leq t
,
by a counting argument there exists
j\in[t+1]
such that
S\cap V_{j}=\emptyset
.
It cannot be that
V_{j}\subseteq C
so,
by connectivity of
G[V_{j}]
, we infer that
C\cap V_{j}=\emptyset
.
Let
v
be an arbitrary vertex from
V_{j}
.
We have that
S
is a
(v,Z)
-separator of size at most
k
.
By
Lemma

3.2
we know that there exists an important
(v,Z)
-separator
S^{\prime}
,
such that
R_{S}(v)\subseteq R_{S^{\prime}}(v)
and
|S^{\prime}|\leq|S|\leq k
.
Since
\mathcal{H}
is hereditary and
V_{j}\subseteq R_{S}(v)
, the subgraph induced by
R_{S^{\prime}}(v)
does not belong to
\mathcal{H}
.
This implies that such a set will be detected by the algorithm.
∎

In the next lemma, we exploit the obstruction
F
to design a branching rule which shaves off some part of the set
S
in the
(\mathcal{H},k)
-separation
(C,S)
covering the set
Z
.
We assume the existence of an algorithm
\mathcal{A}
for
\mathcal{H}
-
deletion
parameterized by the solution size
s
.
We allow
\mathcal{A}
to be a
\beta
-approximation algorithm for some constant
\beta\geq 1
.
Such an algorithm either outputs an
\mathcal{H}
-deletion set of size at most
\beta\cdot s
or correctly concludes that
\mathbf{dn}_{\mathcal{H}}{}(G)>s
.
We describe the running time of
\mathcal{A}
by a function
f(n,s)
, where
n
is the input size.
This function may be purely polynomial and independent of
s
; this will be the case when
\mathcal{H}
-
deletion
admits a constant-factor polynomial-time approximation.
On the other hand, if
\mathcal{H}
-
deletion
admits an exact FPT algorithm, the function
f(n,s)
may be of the form, e.g.,
2^{\mathcal{O}(s)}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
and the approximation factor
\beta
equals 1.

Lemma 4.29
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a hereditary union-closed graph class.
Suppose that
\mathcal{H}
-
deletion
admits a
\beta
-approximation algorithm
\mathcal{A}

(\beta=\mathcal{O}(1)
,
\beta\geq 1)
parameterized by the solution size
s
running in time
f(n,s)
on an
n
-vertex graph, where
f
is a computable function non-decreasing on both coordinates.
Then there is an
f(n,t(t+1))\cdot 2^{\mathcal{O}(t)}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
-time algorithm solving
(\mathcal{H},h)
-
separation finding
for
h(x)=\beta\cdot 2x(2x+1)
.
If
\mathcal{A}
runs in polynomial space, then the latter algorithm does as well.

Proof.

Let
(G,Z,k,t)
be the given instance.
By the problem definition
G[Z]
is connected, so whenever
G
is not connected it suffices to focus on the connected component containing
Z
.
We present a recursive algorithm that works under the assumption that
there exists an
(\mathcal{H},k)
-separation
(C,S)
covering

Z
and outputs an
(\mathcal{H},\beta(t+k)(t+k+1))
-separation weakly covering
Z
(recall that
k\leq t
by the problem definition).
If the algorithm fails to output an
(\mathcal{H},\beta(t+k)(t+k+1))
-separation weakly covering
Z
, we shall conclude that
Z
is
(\mathcal{H},k)
-inseparable.
In the corner case
k=0
it suffices to check whether the connected component containing
Z
induces a graph from
\mathcal{H}
.

We can use
\mathcal{A}
to test whether an
n
-vertex graph belongs to
\mathcal{H}
in time
f(n,0)
.
We execute the algorithm from
Lemma

4.28
in
time
4^{k}\cdot(f(n,0)+n^{\mathcal{O}(1)})
(and polynomial space if
\mathcal{A}
runs in polynomial space).
If it reports that
\mathbf{dn}_{\mathcal{H}}{}(G)\leq t(t+1)
we
invoke the algorithm
\mathcal{A}
to find an
\mathcal{H}
-deletion set
S^{\prime}
of size at most
\beta t(t+1)
, in time
f(n,t(t+1))
.
We then output the
(\mathcal{H},\beta t(t+1))
-separation
(V(G)\setminus S^{\prime},S^{\prime})
which weakly covers
Z
.
Otherwise we obtain a set
F\subseteq V(G)

such that (1)
G[F]\not\in\mathcal{H}
, (2)
G[F]
is connected, (3)
N_{G}[F]\cap Z=\emptyset
, and (4)
|N_{G}(F)|\leq k
.

In such a case we perform branching:
we create
multiple instances of the form
(G-S^{\prime},Z,k^{\prime},t)
where
S^{\prime}
is some
(F,Z)
-separator of size at most
k
and
k^{\prime}<k
.
Note that
Z
belongs to the vertex set of the graph on which we recurse and
\mathbf{tw}_{\mathcal{H}}(G-S)\leq\mathbf{tw}_{\mathcal{H}}(G)\leq t
.
We keep the value of parameter
t
intact so the invariant
k^{\prime}\leq t
is preserved.
If an
(\mathcal{H},\beta(t+k^{\prime})(t+k^{\prime}+1))
-separation
(\widehat{C},\widehat{S})
weakly covering
Z
in
G-S^{\prime}
is found, we output
(\widehat{C},\widehat{S}\cup S^{\prime})
for the original instance
(G,Z,k,t)
.
Observe that in such a case
|\widehat{S}\cup S^{\prime}|\leq\beta(t+k^{\prime})(t+k^{\prime}+1)+k\leq\beta(t+k-1)(t+k)+k\leq\beta(t+k)(t+k+1)
and
N_{G}(\widehat{C})\subseteq\widehat{S}\cup S^{\prime}
, so the solution is valid.
It remains to design a branching rule to ensure that in least one call
(G-S^{\prime},Z,k^{\prime},t)
the set
Z
is
(\mathcal{H},k^{\prime})
-separable in
G-S^{\prime}
.
Then it follows from induction that an
(\mathcal{H},\beta(t+k^{\prime})(t+k^{\prime}+1))
-separation
(\widehat{C},\widehat{S})
weakly covering
Z
in
G-S^{\prime}
will be found.

The branching rule is divided into 2 parts.
In part (a) we consider all the important
(F,Z)
-separators of size at most
k
.
For each such a separator
S^{\prime}
we recurse on the instance
(G-S^{\prime},Z,k-|S^{\prime}|,t)
.
In part (b) we produce only one recursive call:

(G-N_{G}(F),Z,k-1,t)
.
Note that
N_{G}(F)
is an
(F,Z)
-separator of size at most
k
, so this instance obeys the description above.
Next, we advocate the correctness of this rule.
Recall that
(C,S)
refers to an (unknown)
(\mathcal{H},k)
-separation covering the given set
Z
, which we have assumed to exist.

If
F\cap S=\emptyset
, then by connectivity of
G[F]
we obtain that either
F\subseteq C
or
C\cap F=\emptyset
.
The first option is impossible because
G[F]\not\in\mathcal{H}
,
therefore
S
is an
(F,Z)
-separator.
From
Lemma

4.22

we get that there exists an
(\mathcal{H},k)
-separation
(C^{*},S^{*})
covering
Z
and an important
(F,Z)
-separator
S^{\prime}
, such that
S^{\prime}\subseteq S^{*}
.
Furthermore (from the same lemma), we know that
(C^{*},S^{*}\setminus S^{\prime})
is an
(\mathcal{H},k-|S^{\prime}|)
-separation covering
Z
in
G-S^{\prime}
, hence the instance
(G-S^{\prime},Z,k-|S^{\prime}|,t)
admits a solution.
Recall that we have reduced the problem to the case where
G
is connected so
|S^{\prime}|>0
.
Such an important
(F,Z)
-separator
S^{\prime}
is going to be considered during the branching rule, part (a), and in such a case the algorithm returns an
(\mathcal{H},k)
-separation obeying the requirements for the instance
(G-S^{\prime},Z,k-|S^{\prime}|,t)
.

Now consider the case
F\cap S\neq\emptyset
.
This means that the connected component of
F
in
G-N_{G}(F)
contains at least one vertex from
S
and thus
the connected component of
Z

contains at most
k-1
vertices from
S
.
More precisely, let
V_{Z}
denote the vertex set inducing the connected component of
Z
in
G-N_{G}(F)
.
Then
|S\cap V_{Z}|<k
and
(C\cap V_{Z},S\cap V_{Z})
is an
(\mathcal{H},k-1)
-separation covering
Z
in
G[V_{Z}]
, and thus in
G-N_{G}(F)
.
Hence, this case is covered in the part (b) in the branching rule.

Finally, we analyze the running time.
We bound the number of leaves in the recursion tree.
Let
T(k)
denote the maximal number of leaves for a call with parameter
k
– we shall prove by induction that
T(k)\leq 5^{k}
.
We have
T(0)=1
.
In part (a) of the branching rule
we consider all the important
(F,Z)
-separators of size at most
k
and for each such separator
S^{\prime}
we create an instance with parameter value
k-|S^{\prime}|
.
Similarly as in the proof of
Lemma

4.23
we take advantage of
Lemma

4.21
to upper bound the sum of these terms.
The second summand comes from part (b), where we create a single instance with parameter value
k-1
.

\displaystyle T(k)\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{k}(F,Z)}T(k-|S^{\prime}|)+T(k-1)\leq\sum_{S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{k}(F,Z)}5^{k-|S^{\prime}|}+5^{k-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{k}(F,Z)}\left(\frac{5}{4}\right)^{k-|S^{\prime}|}4^{k}\cdot 4^{-|S^{\prime}|}+5^{k-1}\leq\,\left(\frac{5}{4}\right)^{k-1}4^{k}\sum_{S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{k}(F,Z)}4^{-|S^{\prime}|}+5^{k-1}

\displaystyle\leq\,\,

\displaystyle 4\cdot 5^{k-1}+5^{k-1}=5^{k}\quad\text{(Lemma~\ref{lem:important-sum})}

The number of nodes in the recursion tree of depth bounded by
k
is clearly at most
k\cdot T(k)
.
In each node we may invoke the algorithm from
Lemma

4.28
running in time
4^{k}\cdot(f(n,0)+n^{\mathcal{O}(1)})
, the algorithm
\mathcal{A}
running in time
f(n,t(t+1))
, and enumerate the important
(F,Z)
separators of size at most
k
, which takes time
4^{k}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
(
4.20
).
∎

4.2.4
Finding restricted separations

As explained in Section
4.1.2
, for the sake of constructing a tree
\mathcal{H}
-decomposition we need algorithms with a stronger guarantee, formalized as a restricted separation.
We recall the problem statement.

Restricted
(\mathcal{H},h)
-separation finding

Parameter:

t

Input:
Integers
k\leq t
, a graph
G
of
\mathcal{H}
-treewidth bounded by
t
, a connected non-empty subset
Z\subseteq V(G)
, a family
\mathcal{F}
of connected, disjoint,
(\mathcal{H},k)
-inseparable subsets of
V(G)
.

Task:
Either return an
(\mathcal{H},h(t))
-separation
(C,S)
that weakly covers
Z
such that
C
contains at most
t
sets from
\mathcal{F}
, or conclude that
Z
is
(\mathcal{H},k)
-inseparable.

It turns out that as long as we can solve the basic separation problem exactly,
we obtain the stronger guarantee “for free”.

Lemma 4.30
.

For
\mathcal{H}
given by a finite family of forbidden induced subgraphs and function
h(x)=x
,
Restricted
(\mathcal{H},h)
-separation finding
is solvable in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(t)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
and polynomial space.

Proof.

We have
k\leq t
.
By
Lemma

4.23
,
there is an algorithm running in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(k)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
and polynomial space,
which given a non-empty connected set
Z
in a graph
G
, outputs an
(\mathcal{H},k)
-separation
(C,S)
covering
Z
or concludes that
Z
is
(\mathcal{H},k)
-inseparable.
If the set
C
is
(\mathcal{H},k)
-separable,
no subset of
C
can be
(\mathcal{H},k)
-inseparable and the condition required in restricted separation is always satisfied. Hence the output for the non-restricted version of the problem is also valid in the restricted setting.
∎

For other graph classes we present a reduction from
Restricted
(\mathcal{H},2h)
-separation finding
to
(\mathcal{H},h)
-separation finding
that is burdened with a multiplicative factor
2^{\mathcal{O}(t\log t)}
. The following lemma applies to hereditary classes
\mathcal{H}
, even those which are not union-closed.

Lemma 4.31
.

Suppose
(\mathcal{H},h)
-separation finding
admits an algorithm
\mathcal{A}
running in time
f(n,t)

and function
h
satisfies
h(x)\geq x
.
Then
Restricted
(\mathcal{H},2h)
-separation finding
admits an algorithm with running time
2^{\mathcal{O}(t\log t)}\cdot(f(n,t)+n^{\mathcal{O}(1)})
.
If
\mathcal{A}
runs in polynomial space, then the latter algorithm does as well.

Proof.

We shall prove a stronger claim: given an instance
(G,Z,\mathcal{F},k,t)
, within the claimed running time we can either find an
(\mathcal{H},h(t)+k)
-separation weakly covering
Z
and covering at most
k
sets from
\mathcal{F}
, or correctly report that
Z
is
(\mathcal{H},k)
-inseparable.
This is indeed stronger than the lemma statement because
k\leq t\leq h(t)
.

We begin with the description of the algorithm.
Since it suffices to consider the connected component containing
Z
and those sets from
\mathcal{F}
contained in that component, we may assume that
G
is connected.
The algorithm distinguishes two cases.
First, if
|\mathcal{F}|\leq k
then we just execute the
algorithm
\mathcal{A}
with input
(G,Z,k,t)
.
If
|\mathcal{F}|>k

we choose an arbitrary subfamily
\mathcal{F}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{F}
of size
k+1
.
We branch on the choice of
F\in\mathcal{F}_{0}
and the choice of an important
(F,Z)
-separator of size at most
k
.
For each
S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{k}(F,Z)
we invoke a recursive call
(G^{\prime},Z,\mathcal{F}^{\prime},k^{\prime},t)
with

G^{\prime}=G-S^{\prime}
,
k^{\prime}=k-|S^{\prime}|
, and
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
being the subfamily of sets from
\mathcal{F}
that are subsets of
V(G^{\prime})
.
If an
(\mathcal{H},h(t)+k^{\prime})
-separation
(\widehat{C},\widehat{S})
weakly covering
Z
in
G^{\prime}
is found, we return
(\widehat{C},\widehat{S}\cup S^{\prime})
.
By connectivity we know that the separator
S^{\prime}
is non-empty so
k^{\prime}<k
.
Note that we preserve the invariant
k^{\prime}\leq t
.
If we do not have enough budget to cut away any set
F
, the recursive call reports failure.

We prove the correctness of the algorithm by induction on
k
.
Recall the assumption that
G
is connected.
If
k=0
and
\mathcal{F}=\emptyset
the algorithm
\mathcal{A}
gets executed and clearly returns a feasible solution because the restricted condition is trivially satisfied.
If
k=0
and
\mathcal{F}\neq\emptyset
, then we know that
G\not\in\mathcal{H}
because otherwise any subset of
V(G)
would be
(\mathcal{H},k)
-separable.
In this case
Z
is
(\mathcal{H},k)
-inseparable because any
(\mathcal{H},0)
-separation
(C,S)
covering
Z
would need to satisfy
C=V(G)
.
The algorithm will report this fact as there are no important separators of size 0 to perform branching.

If
k>0
and
|\mathcal{F}|\leq k
, then any (unrestricted)
(\mathcal{H},h(t))
-separation found by the algorithm
\mathcal{A}
satisfies the restriction trivially.
Suppose that
k>0
and
|\mathcal{F}|>k
. It is easy to see that the algorithm gives a correct answer when it returns a separation, so it suffices to prove that the algorithm indeed outputs a separation when
Z
is
(\mathcal{H},k)
-separable. Consider
some
(\mathcal{H},k)
-separation
(C,S)
covering
Z
.
Let
\mathcal{F}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{F}
be the chosen subfamily of size
k+1
.
None of the sets from
\mathcal{F}_{0}
can be contained in
C
(as they are
(\mathcal{H},k)
-inseparable) and
|\mathcal{F}_{0}|>|S|
so by connectivity for some
F\in\mathcal{F}_{0}
the set
S
is an
(F,Z)
-separator.
By Lemma
4.22
we know that there exists an
(\mathcal{H},k)
-separation
(C^{*},S^{*})
covering
Z
such that
S^{*}
contains an important
(F,Z)
-separator
S^{\prime}
.
During the branching the algorithm considers the separator
S^{\prime}
and invokes a recursive call
(G^{\prime},Z,\mathcal{F}^{\prime},k^{\prime},t)
with

G^{\prime}=G-S^{\prime}
,
k^{\prime}=k-|S^{\prime}|
, and
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
being the subfamily of sets from
\mathcal{F}
that are subsets of
V(G^{\prime})
.
Then
Z
is
(\mathcal{H},k^{\prime})
-separable in
G^{\prime}
and by induction we know that the algorithm will output some
(\mathcal{H},h(t)+k^{\prime})
-separation
(\widehat{C},\widehat{S})

weakly covering
Z
in
G^{\prime}

such that there are at most
k^{\prime}
sets from
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
contained in
\widehat{C}
.
Furthermore,
N_{G}(\widehat{C})\subseteq\widehat{S}\cup S^{\prime}
and no set from
\mathcal{F}\setminus\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
is contained in
\widehat{C}
so

(\widehat{C},\widehat{S}\cup S^{\prime})
is an
(\mathcal{H},h(t)+k)
-separation weakly covering
Z
in
G
and covering at most
k^{\prime}\leq k
sets from
\mathcal{F}
.

Finally, we analyze the running time.
The time spent in each recursive node is proportional to the number of direct recursive calls, modulo a polynomial factor, due to Theorem
4.20
. We will bound the number of leaves in the recursion tree similarly as in
Lemma

4.23
.
Let
T(k)
denote the maximal number of leaves for a call with argument
k
.
We prove by induction that
T(k)\leq(k+1)!\cdot 4^{k}
, which holds for
k=0
.
For each recursive call, as described above, we estimate

T(k)\leq\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}_{0}}\sum_{S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{k}(F,Z)}T(k-|S^{\prime}|)\leq k!\cdot 4^{k}\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}_{0}}\sum_{S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{k}(F,Z)}4^{-|S^{\prime}|}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{Lemma }\ref{lem:important-sum}}}{{\leq}}(k+1)!\cdot 4^{k}.

The number of calls to
\mathcal{A}
is thus at most
T(k)
.
Since the depth of the recursion tree is
k
, we obtain a bound of
k\cdot(k+1)!\cdot 4^{k}
on the total number of recursive nodes.
Together with the assumption
k\leq t
, we obtain the claimed running time
2^{\mathcal{O}(t\log t)}\cdot(f(n,t)+n^{\mathcal{O}(1)})
.
The important separators can be enumerated using only polynomial space, so whenever
\mathcal{A}
runs in polynomial space then the entire algorithm does.
∎

4.2.5
Disconnected obstructions

So far we have focused on union-closed graph classes, so we could take advantage of
Lemma

4.27
(with the exception of classes given by forbidden induced subgraphs in
Lemma

4.23
).
If the class
\mathcal{H}
is given by a family
\mathcal{F}
of forbidden (topological) minors, then
\mathcal{H}
is union-closed if all graphs in
\mathcal{F}
are connected.
We shall now explain how to reduce the general problem to this case.
We define the class
\mathcal{D(H)}
to be the closure of
\mathcal{H}
under taking disjoint unions of graphs,
i.e.,
G\in\mathcal{D(H)}
if each connected component of
G
belongs to
\mathcal{H}
.
Observe that in the definition of
\mathcal{H}
-elimination forest and tree
\mathcal{H}
-decomposition we only require that each base component belongs to
\mathcal{H}
.
If a base component
C
is disconnected we can make several copies of its bag, one for each connected component of
C
.
Therefore we are only interested in checking whether some connected graphs belong to
\mathcal{H}
and for the sake of obtaining decompositions there is no difference in studying
\mathcal{H}
or
\mathcal{D(H)}
.

Observation 4.32
.

An
\mathcal{H}
-elimination forest (resp. tree
\mathcal{H}
-decomposition) of
G
can be transformed into an
\mathcal{D(H)}
-elimination forest (resp. tree
\mathcal{D(H)}
-decomposition) of
G
of the same width, in polynomial time.
In particular,
\mathbf{ed}_{\mathcal{H}}(G)=\mathbf{ed}_{\mathcal{D(H)}}(G)
and
\mathbf{tw}_{\mathcal{H}}(G)=\mathbf{tw}_{\mathcal{D(H)}}(G)
.

Below we explain that given
\mathcal{H}
defined via a finite family of forbidden (topological) minors, we can construct an analogous family for
\mathcal{D(H)}
, in which all the graphs are connected.

Lemma 4.33
.

There exists an algorithm that, for
\mathcal{H}
given by a finite family of forbidden (topological) minors
\mathcal{F}
, generates the family
\mathcal{D(F)}
of
forbidden (topological) minors for
\mathcal{D(H)}
.
All the graphs in
\mathcal{D(F)}
are connected.

Proof.

Bulian and Dawar
[
20
]
have presented such an algorithm for non-topological minors and we summarize it below.
It holds that
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{D(H)}
as long as all the graphs in
\mathcal{F}
are connected.
If
H\in\mathcal{F}
is not connected
and a connected graph
G
contains
H
as a minor, then

G
also contains a minor being a connected supergraph of
H
on vertex set
V(H)
.
For each
H\in\mathcal{F}
we generate all connected supergraphs of
H
on vertex set
V(H)
, and add them to
\mathcal{D(F)}
. Afterwards we trim the family to its minor-minimal elements, to obtain the desired family of forbidden minors for
\mathcal{D}(H)
.

With a small modification, the same argument also works for topological minors.
If
H\in\mathcal{F}
consists of multiple connected components
H_{1},\dots,H_{m}
,
we consider the family
\mathcal{T}_{m}
of all trees
T
such that
\{1,\dots,m\}\subseteq V(T)
and all vertices apart from
\{1,\dots,m\}
have degree at least 3.
Clearly, for
T\in\mathcal{T}_{m}
it holds that
|V(T)|\leq 2m
, so the family
\mathcal{T}_{m}
is finite.
Now consider a family
\mathcal{D}(H)
given by choosing
T\in\mathcal{T}_{m}
, replacing the vertices
\{1,\dots,m\}
with components
H_{1},\dots,H_{m}
,
so that if
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
is an endpoint of an edge from
E(T)
, it gets replaced by some vertex from
H_{i}
or a vertex created by subdividing an edge in
H_{i}
.
Then
\mathcal{D}(H)
is also finite and
if a connected graph
G
contains
H
as a topological minor, then

G
also contains a topological minor from
\mathcal{D}(H)
.
The family
\mathcal{D(F)}
is given by the family of topological-minor-minimal graphs in the union of
\mathcal{D}(H)
over
H\in\mathcal{F}
.
∎

We remark that when
\mathcal{H}
is not closed under disjoint unions of graphs, we do not exploit the decompositions for solving the respective vertex deletion problems, as these become para-NP-hard (see Section
6.2
).
However, we provided a general way of constructing decompositions for the sake of completeness and because the resulting algorithms to approximate elimination distance and
\mathcal{H}
-treewidth are of independent interest.

4.3
Summary of the decomposition results

Supplied with the algorithms for
(\mathcal{H},h)
-separation finding
and
Restricted
(\mathcal{H},h)
-separation finding
, we can plug them into the framework from Section
4.1
.
Each result below follows from the combination of a respective algorithm for
(\mathcal{H},h)
-separation finding
with a procedure to build either an
\mathcal{H}
-elimination forest or a tree
\mathcal{H}
-decomposition.
Whenever the first algorithm runs in polynomial space, we formulate an additional claim about constructing the
\mathcal{H}
-elimination forest in polynomial space, but with a slightly worse approximation guarantee.
We begin with a formalization of
1.2
, which allows us to cover several graph classes in a unified way, and then move on to applications.

Theorem 4.34
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a hereditary union-closed graph class.
Suppose that
\mathcal{H}
-
deletion
admits an exact or
\mathcal{O}(1)
-approximate algorithm
\mathcal{A}
parameterized by the solution size
s
running in time
f(n,s)
on an
n
-vertex graph, where
f
is a computable function non-decreasing on both coordinates.
There exist algorithms which given a graph
G
perform the following:

a)

if
\mathbf{tw}_{\mathcal{H}}(G)\leq k
, the algorithm runs in time
f\left(n,k(k+1)\right)\cdot 2^{\mathcal{O}(k\log k)}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
and returns a tree
\mathcal{H}
-decomposition of width
\mathcal{O}(k^{5})
,

b)

if
\mathbf{ed}_{\mathcal{H}}(G)\leq k
, the algorithm runs in time
\left(f(n,k(k+1))\cdot 2^{\mathcal{O}(k)}+2^{\mathcal{O}(k^{2})}\right)\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
and returns an
\mathcal{H}
-elimination forest of depth
\mathcal{O}(k^{3})
,

c)

(only applicable when
\mathcal{A}
works in polynomial space) if
\mathbf{ed}_{\mathcal{H}}(G)\leq k
, the algorithm runs in time
f\left(n,k(k+1)\right)\cdot 2^{\mathcal{O}(k)}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
and polynomial space, and returns an
\mathcal{H}
-elimination forest of depth
\mathcal{O}(k^{4}\log^{3/2}k)
.

Proof.

From
Lemma

4.29
we obtain an algorithm for
(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{O}(t^{2}))
-separation finding
running in time

g(n,t)=f(n,t(t+1))\cdot 2^{\mathcal{O}(t)}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.
If
\mathcal{A}
runs in polynomial space then the algorithm above does as well and we can plug it to
Lemma

4.11
to prove the claim (c) with running time
g(n,k)\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.
Similarly, we obtain (b) via
Lemma

4.12
to get running time
(g(n,k)+2^{\mathcal{O}(k^{2})})\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.
We construct an algorithm for
Restricted
(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{O}(t^{2}))
-separation finding
via
Lemma

4.31
at the expense of multiplying the running time
g(n,t)
by
2^{\mathcal{O}(t\log t)}
.
Then the claim (a) follows from
Lemma

4.19
.
∎

Instead of formulating a lengthy theorem statement about numerous graph classes, we gather the detailed list of results in
Table

2
and provide a combined proof for all of them below.
The cases of bipartite graphs and classes given by a finite family of forbidden induced subgraphs are amenable to
4.34
, but we provide specialized algorithms achieving better running times and approximation guarantees.

Table 2
:
Summary of decomposition results. Each result is of the form: there is an algorithm that, given graph
G
satisfying
\mathbf{ed}_{\mathcal{H}}(G)\leq k
(resp.
\mathbf{tw}_{\mathcal{H}}(G)\leq k
), runs in time
f(k)\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
, and returns an
\mathcal{H}
-elimination forest of depth
h(k)
(resp. a tree
\mathcal{H}
-decomposition of width
h(k)
).
One can read the bounds on
h
(top) and
f
(bottom) in
the corresponding cell.
For bipartite graphs and
h(k)=\mathcal{O}(k^{3}\log^{3/2}k)
we obtain
f=\mathcal{O}(1)
as the algorithm runs in polynomial time.

\begin{array}[]{|c|c|c|c|c|}\hline\cr\text{class }\mathcal{H}&&\mathbf{tw}_{\mathcal{H}}&\mathbf{ed}_{\mathcal{H}}&\mathbf{ed}_{\mathcal{H}}\text{ in poly-space}\\
\hline\cr\text{bipartite}&h&\mathcal{O}(k^{3})&\mathcal{O}(k^{2})&\mathcal{O}(k^{3}\log^{3/2}k)\\
&f&2^{\mathcal{O}(k\log k)}&2^{\mathcal{O}(k^{2})}&\mathcal{O}(1)\\
\hline\cr\text{forbidden induced}&h&\mathcal{O}(k^{3})&\mathcal{O}(k^{2})&\mathcal{O}(k^{3}\log^{3/2}k)\\
\text{subgraphs}&f&2^{\mathcal{O}(k)}&2^{\mathcal{O}(k^{2})}&2^{\mathcal{O}(k)}\\
\hline\cr\text{chordal}&h&\mathcal{O}(k^{5})&\mathcal{O}(k^{3})&\mathcal{O}(k^{4}\log^{3/2}k)\\
&f&2^{\mathcal{O}(k^{2}\log k)}&2^{\mathcal{O}(k^{2}\log k)}&2^{\mathcal{O}(k^{2}\log k)}\\
\hline\cr\text{(proper) interval}&h&\mathcal{O}(k^{5})&\mathcal{O}(k^{3})&\mathcal{O}(k^{4}\log^{3/2}k)\\
&f&2^{\mathcal{O}(k\log k)}&2^{\mathcal{O}(k^{2})}&2^{\mathcal{O}(k)}\\
\hline\cr\text{bipartite permutation}&h&\mathcal{O}(k^{5})&\mathcal{O}(k^{3})&\mathcal{O}(k^{4}\log^{3/2}k)\\
&f&2^{\mathcal{O}(k\log k)}&2^{\mathcal{O}(k^{2})}&2^{\mathcal{O}(k)}\\
\hline\cr\text{(linear) rankwidth $\leq 1$}&h&\mathcal{O}(k^{5})&\mathcal{O}(k^{3})&\mathcal{O}(k^{4}\log^{3/2}k)\\
&f&2^{\mathcal{O}(k^{2})}&2^{\mathcal{O}(k^{2})}&2^{\mathcal{O}(k^{2})}\\
\hline\cr\text{planar}&h&\mathcal{O}(k^{5})&\mathcal{O}(k^{3})&-\\
&f&2^{\mathcal{O}(k^{2}\log k)}&2^{\mathcal{O}(k^{2}\log k)}&\text{}\\
\hline\cr\text{forbidden}&h&\mathcal{O}(k^{5})&\mathcal{O}(k^{3})&-\\
\text{minors}&f&2^{k^{\mathcal{O}(1)}}&2^{k^{\mathcal{O}(1)}}&\text{}\\
\hline\cr\text{forbidden topological}&h&\mathcal{O}(k^{5})&\mathcal{O}(k^{3})&-\\
\text{minors}&f&\text{FPT}&\text{FPT}&\text{}\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Theorem 4.35
.

For each cell in
Table

2
there exists an algorithm
that, given graph
G
satisfying
\mathbf{ed}_{\mathcal{H}}(G)\leq k
(resp.
\mathbf{tw}_{\mathcal{H}}(G)\leq k
), runs in time
f(k)\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
, and returns an
\mathcal{H}
-elimination forest of depth
h(k)
(resp. a tree
\mathcal{H}
-decomposition of width
h(k)
).
The algorithms specified in the last column work in polynomial space.

Proof.

For
\mathcal{H}=\mathsf{bipartite}
, we take advantage of the polynomial-time algorithm for
(\mathcal{H},2t)
-
separation finding
from
Lemma

4.24
.
To compute an
\mathcal{H}
-elimination forest we combine it with Lemmas
4.11
and
4.12
.
Note that in the first case we achieve a polynomial-time algorithm.
From Lemmas
4.24
and
4.31
we obtain an algorithm for
Restricted
(\mathcal{H},4t)
-separation finding
running in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(t\log t)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
and polynomial space.
The algorithm computing a tree
\mathcal{H}
-decomposition follows then from Lemma
4.19
.

Let now
\mathcal{H}
be given by a finite family of forbidden induced subgraphs, not necessarily connected.
The algorithms for computing an
\mathcal{H}
-elimination forest are obtained by pipelining the algorithm for
(\mathcal{H},t)
-
separation finding
from Lemma
4.23
, running in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(t)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
and polynomial space, with Lemmas
4.11
and
4.12
, respectively.
For the case of a tree
\mathcal{H}
-decomposition, we supply Lemma
4.19
with the algorithm from Lemma
4.30
.

The remaining results are obtained by supplying

4.34
with known algorithms for
\mathcal{H}
-
deletion
, either approximate or exact, parameterized by the solution size.

First let us consider
\mathcal{H}=\mathsf{chordal}
.
The
Chordal deletion
problem admits an FPT algorithm parameterized by the solution size
s
running in time
f(n,s)=2^{\mathcal{O}(s\log s)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}

[
26
]
and polynomial space
[
23
]
.
The claim follows from plugging this algorithm to
4.34
.

For
