\mathcal{H}\in\{\mathsf{interval},\mathsf{proper}\,\,\mathsf{interval}\}
, we take advantage of the existing constant-factor approximation algorithms for
\mathcal{H}
-
deletion

[
21
,
105
]
.
Pipelining them with
4.34
yields the claim.
The same argument works for the class of bipartite permutation graphs, for which we can also use a constant-factor approximation algorithm
[
106
]
.

When
\mathcal{H}
is a class of graphs of rankwidth at most 1 or linear rankwidth at most 1 (these graphs are also called distance-hereditary), we use algorithms for
\mathcal{H}
-
deletion
, parameterized by the solution size
s
, running in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(s)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}

[
37
,
71
]
.
Both algorithms work in polynomial space
[
76
]
.

For
\mathcal{H}=\mathsf{planar}
, we use the known algorithm for
Vertex planarization
parameterized by the solution size
s
, running in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(s\log s)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}

[
68
]
.
This algorithm however does not work in polynomial space so we do not populate the last cell in the row.

As a more general case, consider
\mathcal{H}
given by a finite family of forbidden minors.

4.34
can be applied directly only when all the graphs in this family are connected as otherwise
\mathcal{H}
might not be union-closed.
However, by

4.32
and
Lemma

4.33
the general problem of computing an
\mathcal{H}
-elimination forest (or a tree
\mathcal{H}
-decomposition) can be reduced to the case where all the forbidden minors are connected.
Therefore, we can employ
4.34
in combination with the algorithm for
\mathcal{H}
-
deletion
, parameterized by the solution size
s
, running in time
2^{s^{\mathcal{O}(1)}}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}

[
102
]
.
Analogous reasoning applies when
\mathcal{H}
is given by a finite family of forbidden topological minors.
Here we rely on the FPT algorithm for
\mathcal{H}
-
deletion

[
47
]
with a computable yet unspecified dependency on the parameter, so we do not specify the function
f
in the table.
∎

5
Solving vertex-deletion problems

We move on to solving particular vertex-deletion problems parameterized by
\mathbf{tw}_{\mathcal{H}}
or
\mathbf{ed}_{\mathcal{H}}
.
In particular, we will be interested in
\mathcal{H}
-
deletion
parameterized by graph measures related to
\mathcal{H}
. We begin by presenting preliminaries specific for solving vertex-deletions problems in Section
5.1
. In Section
5.2
we show how several existing algorithms can be adapted to work with the parameterizations
\mathbf{tw}_{\mathcal{H}}
and
\mathbf{ed}_{\mathcal{H}}
, leading to algorithms solving
Odd Cycle Transversal
and
Vertex Cover
. In Section
5.3
we present a meta-algorithm and apply it to several problems using new or known bounds on the sizes of minimal representatives of suitable equivalence classes.

5.1
Preliminaries for solving vertex-deletion problems

Let
T
be a rooted tree. We denote the subtree of
T
rooted at
t\in V(T)
by
T_{t}
. The distance (in terms of the number of edges) from
t\in V(T)
to the root of
T
is denoted by
0pt_{T}(t)
. The height of a rooted tree
T
is the maximum number of edges on a path from the root to a leaf, we denote it by
0pt(T)
. For an
\mathcal{H}
-elimination forest
(T,\chi)
, let
V_{t}=\bigcup\{\chi(t^{\prime})\mid t\text{ is an ancestor of }t^{\prime}\}
.

We begin with a simple observation that will play an important role in several arguments.

Lemma 5.1
.

Suppose
\mathcal{H}
is union-closed,
X\subseteq V(G)
is a minimum
\mathcal{H}
-deletion set in graph
G
and
Z\subseteq V(G)
satisfies
G[Z]\in\mathcal{H}
. Then
|X\cap Z|\leq|N(Z)|
.

Proof.

Suppose that
|X\cap Z|>|N(Z)|
.
Consider an alternative solution candidate
X^{\prime}=(X\setminus Z)\cup N(Z)
.
The graph
G-(N[Z]\cup X)
is an induced subgraph of
G-X
, so it belongs to
\mathcal{H}
, because the class is hereditary.
Furthermore,
G[Z]\in\mathcal{H}
and
G-X^{\prime}
is a disjoint union of these graphs, so
X^{\prime}
is an
\mathcal{H}
-deletion set.
To compare the sizes of
X
and
X^{\prime}
,
observe that when defining
X^{\prime}

we have removed all vertices from
X\cap Z
and added at most
|N(Z)|
new vertices, therefore
|X^{\prime}|<|X|
.
This contradicts that
X
is a minimum deletion set.
∎

It is particularly useful when we are guaranteed that
Z
has a small neighborhood.
This is exactly the case for the base components in both types of decompositions: the base components of an
\mathcal{H}
-elimination forest of depth
k
(resp. tree
\mathcal{H}
-decomposition of width
k-1
) have neighborhood of size at most
k
.
We could thus assume that the intersection of any minimum
\mathcal{H}
-deletion set with any base component is not too large.

Corollary 5.2
.

Suppose
\mathcal{H}
is closed under disjoint unions of graphs,
X\subseteq V(G)
is a minimum
\mathcal{H}
-deletion set in
G
, and
Z\subseteq V(G)
is a base component of an
\mathcal{H}
-elimination forest of depth
k
or a tree
\mathcal{H}
-decomposition of width
k-1
.
Then
|X\cap Z|\leq k
.

Nice decompositions

We now introduce a standardized form of tree
\mathcal{H}
-decompositions which is useful to streamline dynamic-programming algorithms. It generalizes the nice (standard) tree decompositions which are often used in the literature and were introduced by Kloks
[
74
]
.
A similar construction has been introduced by Eiben et al.
[
36
]
, however we treat the leaves in a slightly different way for our convenience.

Definition 5.3
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a graph class. A tree
\mathcal{H}
-decomposition
(T,\chi,L)
is
nice
if the tree
T
is rooted at a vertex
r
such that
\chi(r)\cap L=\emptyset
and the following properties hold in addition to those of Definition
3.4
:

1.

Each node of
T
has at most two children.

2.

If
t\in V(T)
has two distinct children
c_{1},c_{2}
, then
\chi(t)=\chi(c_{1})=\chi(c_{2})
. (This implies that
\chi(t)\cap L=\chi(c_{1})\cap L=\chi(c_{2})\cap L=\emptyset
.)

3.

If node
t
has a single child
c
, then one of the following holds:

(a)

There is a vertex
v\in V(G)\setminus L
such that
\chi(t)=\chi(c)\cup\{v\}
.

(b)

There is a vertex
v\in V(G)\setminus L
such that
\chi(t)=\chi(c)\setminus\{v\}
.

(c)

The node
c
is a leaf and
\chi(t)=\chi(c)\setminus L
.

As our approach to dynamic programming over a nice tree
\mathcal{H}
-decomposition handles all nodes with a single child in the same way, regardless of whether a vertex is being introduced or forgotten compared to its child, we do not use the terminology of
introduce
or
forget
node in this work.

We are going to show that any tree
\mathcal{H}
-decomposition can efficiently be transformed into a nice one without increasing its width.
The analogous construction for standard tree decompositions is well-known and we shall use it as a black box.
In order to ensure that this process preserves existing bags,
we will rely on the following observation.

Observation 5.4
.

Suppose that
(T,\chi)
is a tree decomposition of
G
and the vertex set
A\subseteq V(G)
forms a clique.
Then there exists a node
t\in V(T)
such that
A\subseteq\chi(t)
.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a graph class. There is an algorithm that, given an
n
-vertex graph
G
and a tree
\mathcal{H}
-decomposition
(T,\chi,L)
of
G
of width
k
, runs in time
\mathcal{O}(n+|V(T)|\cdot k^{2})
and outputs a
nice
tree
\mathcal{H}
-decomposition
(T^{\prime},\chi^{\prime},L)
of
G
of width at most
k
satisfying
|V(T^{\prime})|=\mathcal{O}(kn)
.

Proof.

Kloks
[
74
]
provided an algorithm for turning a tree decomposition into a nice one with the same bounds as in the statement of the lemma.
The (standard) nice tree decomposition satisfies Definition
5.3
with
L=\emptyset
.

Let
G_{0}=G-L
be the graph
G
without the base components.
The tree
\mathcal{H}
-decomposition
(T,\chi,L)
induces a (standard) tree decomposition
(T_{0},\chi_{0})
of
G_{0}
of the same width.
We would like to turn
(T_{0},\chi_{0})
into a nice decomposition and then append the base components back.
However, we would like to keep the bags to which the base components are connected.
In order to ensure this, consider a graph
G^{\prime}_{0}
obtained from
G_{0}
by adding an edge between each pair of vertices that reside in a common bag of
(T_{0},\chi_{0})
.
Note that
(T_{0},\chi_{0})
is a valid tree decomposition of
G^{\prime}_{0}
.

By the known construction
[
32
,
74
]
,
we can build a nice tree decomposition
(T^{\prime}_{0},\chi^{\prime}_{0})
of
G^{\prime}_{0}

with
\mathcal{O}(k\cdot|V(G_{0}^{\prime})|)
nodes and width
k
, in time
\mathcal{O}(n+|V(T_{0})|\cdot k^{2})
.
If a set of vertices
B\subseteq V(G_{0})
forms a bag in
(T_{0},\chi_{0})
, then
B
is a clique in
G_{0}^{\prime}
.
By Observation
5.4
, there must be a bag in
(T^{\prime}_{0},\chi^{\prime}_{0})
that contains
B
.

Let
C
be a base component in
(T,\chi,L)
, that resides in the bag of node
t\in V(T)
.
By the observation above, there is a node
t^{\prime}\in V(T^{\prime}_{0})
, such that
N(C)\subseteq\chi^{\prime}_{0}(t^{\prime})
.
For each base component
C
we proceed as follows: (1) identify such a node
t_{C}\in V(T^{\prime}_{0})
, (2) create 2 copies
t_{1},t_{2}
of
t_{C}
, (3) move the original children (if any) of
t_{C}
to be children of
t_{1}
, (4) make
t_{C}
the parent of
t_{1},t_{2}
, (5) create a child node
t_{3}
of
t_{2}
with a bag
\chi_{0}^{\prime}(t_{C})\cup C
.
Such a modification of a nice tree
\mathcal{H}
-decomposition preserves the conditions (1-3) and never modifies the bag of the root node.
For each base component we create only a constant number of new nodes, so the upper bound is preserved.
∎

To exploit the separators which are encoded in a tree
\mathcal{H}
-decomposition, the following definition is useful.

Definition 5.6
.

A
tri-separation
in a graph
G
is a partition
(A,X,B)
of
V(G)
such that no vertex in
A
is adjacent to any vertex of
B
. The set
X
is the
separator
corresponding to the tri-separation. The
order
of the tri-separation is defined as
|X|
.

The following notions will be used to extract tri-separations from rooted tree decompositions. Recall that
T_{t}
denotes the subtree of
t
rooted at
T
.

Definition 5.7
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a graph class and let
(T,\chi,L)
be a nice tree
\mathcal{H}
-decomposition of a graph
G
, rooted at some node
r
. For each node
t\in V(T)
of the decomposition, we define the functions
\pi_{T,\chi},\kappa_{T,\chi}\colon V(T)\to 2^{V(G)}
:

1.

For a non-root node
t
with parent
s
, we define
\pi_{T,\chi}(t):=\chi(s)
. For
r
we set
\pi_{T,\chi}(r)=\emptyset
.

2.

For an arbitrary node
t
, we define
\kappa_{T,\chi}(t):=(\bigcup_{t^{\prime}\in T_{t}}\chi(t^{\prime}))\setminus\pi(t)
.

We omit the subscripts
T,\chi
when they are clear from context.

Intuitively,
\pi(t)
is the bag of the parent of
t
(if there is one) and
\kappa(t)
consists of those vertices that occur in bags in the subtree
T_{t}
but not in the parent bag of
t
. The following observations about
\kappa
will be useful later on.

Observation 5.8
.

If
(T,\chi,L)
is a nice tree
\mathcal{H}
-decomposition of a graph
G
, then the following holds.

1.

For the root
r
of the decomposition tree,
\kappa(r)=V(G)
.

2.

If node
t^{\prime}
is a child of node
t
, then
\kappa(t^{\prime})\subseteq\kappa(t)
.

3.

If
c_{1},c_{2}
are distinct children of a node
t
, then
\kappa(c_{1})\cap\kappa(c_{2})=\emptyset
.

Tri-separations can be deduced from tree decompositions using
\kappa
and
\pi
.

Observation 5.9
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a graph class and let
(T,\chi,L)
be a nice tree
\mathcal{H}
-decomposition of graph
G
, where
t
is rooted at some node
r
. Let
t\in V(T)
, let
A:=\kappa(t)
and let
X:=\chi(t)\cap\pi(t)
. Then
(A,X,V(G)\setminus(A\cup X))
is a tri-separation in
G
.

5.2
Incorporating hybrid parameterizations into existing algorithms

5.2.1
Odd cycle transversal

In this section we show how to obtain an FPT algorithm for the
Odd cycle transversal (oct)
problem when given either a
\mathsf{bip}
-elimination forest or a tree
\mathsf{bip}
-decomposition.
In the
Odd cycle transversal
problem we are given a graph
G
, and ask for the (size of a) minimum vertex set
S\subseteq V(G)
such that
G-S
is bipartite. Such a set
S
is called an odd cycle transversal. We introduce some more terminology. Recall that a graph is bipartite if and only if it admits a proper 2-coloring, this 2-coloring is referred to as a bipartition. For a bipartite graph
G
we say that
A,B\subseteq V(G)
occur in opposite sides of a bipartition of
G
, if there is a proper 2-coloring
c
of
G
such that
A\subseteq c^{-1}(1)
and
B\subseteq c^{-1}(2)
. Another characterization is that a graph is bipartite if and only if it does not contain an odd cycle, explaining the name
Odd cycle transversal
.

In most classical branching algorithms on elimination forests or dynamic-programming algorithms on tree decompositions, some trivial constant time computation is done in the leaves. In our setting we do more work in the leaves, without changing the dependency on the parameter.

Given a
\mathsf{bip}
-elimination forest

The algorithms we present sometimes need to be able to decide that an instance has no solution and then the algorithm should return
\bot
. Our union operator
\cup
has the additional feature that it propagates this information, that is,
A\cup B=\bot
if
A=\bot
or
B=\bot
.
Before we present the algorithm, we introduce the following problem that will be used as a subroutine later on.

Annotated bipartite coloring (abc)

Input:
A bipartite graph
G
, two sets
B_{1},B_{2}\subseteq V(G)
, and an integer
k
.

Task:
Return a minimum-cardinality set
X\subseteq V(G)
such that
G-X
has a bipartition with
B_{1}\setminus X
and
B_{2}\setminus X
on opposite sides, or return
\bot
if no such
X
exists of size at most
k
.

The sets
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
can be seen as precolored vertices that any coloring after deletion of some vertices should respect. The problem is solvable in polynomial time.

Lemma 5.10

(
[
32
, Lemma 4.15]
)
.

Annotated bipartite coloring
can be solved in
\mathcal{O}(k(n+m))
time.

We present a branching algorithm for computing an odd cycle transversal of minimum size in Algorithm
1
. The general idea is to branch on the top-most vertex
t
of a
\mathsf{bip}
-elimination forest, and recursively call annotated subinstances. Since each edge has an ancestor-descendant relation in the elimination forest, we only need to annotate the ancestors of
t
to be able to solve the problem. In the case of
oct
, a vertex
v\in V(G)
is either in the deletion set or in one of two color classes in the resulting bipartite graph. We either return a minimum odd cycle transversal that respects the annotations, or conclude that they cannot lead to an optimal solution for the initial graph. The idea of branching on a top-most vertex of an elimination forest has been used by, e.g., Chen et al.
[
28
]
to show that
Dominating set
is FPT when parameterized by treedepth. They also provide branching strategies for
q
-coloring
and
Vertex cover
.

Algorithm 1

oct
(Graph
G
,
t\in V(T)
,
\mathsf{bip}
-elimination forest
(T,\chi)
,
(S_{1},S_{2},S_{X})
)

1:

2:

(T,\chi)
is a
\mathsf{bip}
-elimination forest of
G
that consists of a single tree.

3:

S=S_{1}\cup S_{2}\cup S_{X}=\{\chi(t^{\prime})\mid t^{\prime}\text{ is a proper ancestor of }t\}
.

4:

S_{1}
,
S_{2}
, and
S_{X}
are pairwise disjoint.

5:

6:

An odd cycle transversal
X_{t}\subseteq V_{t}
of
G[V_{t}\cup S_{1}\cup S_{2}]
of minimum size such that (1)
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
are in opposite sides of a bipartition of
G[V_{t}\cup S_{1}\cup S_{2}]-X_{t}
and (2)
|X_{t}\cap\chi(t^{\prime})|\leq 0pt_{T}(t^{\prime})
for each leaf
t^{\prime}\in V(T_{t})
. If no such
X_{t}
exists, then return
\bot
.

7:

if

t
is a leaf
then

8:

if

\exists_{u,w\in S_{1}}uw\in E(G)\vee\exists_{u,w\in S_{2}}uw\in E(G)

then

9:

return

\bot

10:

else

11:

return

abc
(G[\chi(t)],N_{G}(S_{1})\cap\chi(t),N_{G}(S_{2})\cap\chi(t),0pt_{T}(t))

12:

else

13:

Let
U_{t}
be the children of
t
.

14:

Let
X_{X}=\chi(t)\cup\bigcup_{t^{\prime}\in U_{t}}\textsc{oct}(G,t^{\prime},(T,\chi),(S_{1},S_{2},S_{X}\cup\chi(t)))
.

15:

Let
X_{1}=\bigcup_{t^{\prime}\in U_{t}}\textsc{oct}(G,t^{\prime},(T,\chi),(S_{1}\cup\chi(t),S_{2},S_{X}))
.

16:

Let
X_{2}=\bigcup_{t^{\prime}\in U_{t}}\textsc{oct}(G,t^{\prime},(T,\chi),(S_{1},S_{2}\cup\chi(t),S_{X}))
.

17:

return
smallest of
X_{X},X_{1},X_{2}
that is not
\bot
, or
\bot
otherwise.

Theorem 5.11
.

Odd cycle traversal
can be solved in time
\mathcal{O}(3^{d}\cdot d^{2}\cdot(n+m))
time and polynomial space when given a
\mathsf{bip}
-elimination forest of depth
d
.

Proof.

Let
(F,\chi)
be an
\mathsf{bip}
-elimination forest of depth
d
of the input graph
G
. We solve
Odd cycle transversal
by taking the union of
oct
(G[V_{r_{T}}],r_{T},(T,\chi_{T}),(\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset))
over all trees
T
in the forest
F
. Here
r_{T}\in V(T)
is the root of
T
, and
\chi_{T}
is the function
\chi
restricted to
V(T)
. To see that this solves the problem, by Corollary
5.2
any optimal odd cycle transversal
Y\subseteq V(G)
of
G
would remove at most
0pt_{T}(t^{\prime})
vertices from
\chi(t^{\prime})
for every leaf
t^{\prime}\in V(T)
.
We argue the correctness and running time of Algorithm
1
by induction on
0pt(T_{t})
. Consider a call
oct
(G,t,(T,\chi),(S_{1},S_{2},S_{X}))
that satisfies the input requirements.

If
0pt(T_{t})=0
, then
t
is a leaf and
G[\chi(t)]
is a bipartite base component. For any set
Y_{t}\subseteq V_{t}
that satisfies the output requirements, we must have that
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
are independent sets. Thus if this is not the case, no such
Y_{t}
exists and hence we can return
\bot
. Otherwise, in a proper 2-coloring of the remaining graph, every vertex in
N_{G}(S_{1})\cap\chi(t)
that is not deleted by
Y_{t}
needs to get the color opposite to
S_{1}
. Similarly, every vertex in
N_{G}(S_{2})\cap\chi(t)
that is not deleted by
Y_{t}
needs to get the a color opposite to
S_{2}
. Therefore, any solution to the annotated
oct
problem also solves the
Annotated bipartite coloring
instance on
G[\chi(t)]
with precolored vertex sets
N_{G}(S_{1})\cap\chi(t)
and
N_{G}(S_{2})\cap\chi(t)
with budget
0pt_{T}(t)
. Conversely, for any solution
X
to the
abc
instance, the graph
G[\chi(t)]-X
has a proper 2-coloring with
N_{G}(S_{1})\cap\chi(t)
and
N_{G}(S_{2})\cap\chi(t)
in opposite color classes. By flipping the color classes if needed, we may assume
N_{G}(S_{1})\cap\chi(t)
is colored
2
and
N_{G}(S_{2})\cap\chi(t)
is colored
1
. Since
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
are independent sets, we can extend this
2
-coloring of
G[\chi(t)]-X
to a
2
-coloring of
G[V_{t}\cup S_{1}\cup S_{2}]-X=G[\chi(t)\cup S_{1}\cup S_{2}]-X
by simply assigning
S_{1}
color
1
and
S_{2}
color
2
. Hence the call to
abc
results in the correct answer to the annotated
oct
problem.

In the case that
t
is not a leaf, fix a solution
Y_{t}\subseteq V_{t}
that satisfies the output requirements if there is one; otherwise let
Y_{t}=\bot
. The single vertex in
\chi(t)
must either be in the odd cycle transversal, or in one of two color classes of the remaining bipartition. We branch on each of these cases, and construct solutions
X_{X}
,
X_{1}
, and
X_{2}
. Initially we add
\chi(t)
to the transversal
X_{X}
, in the other cases
\chi(t)
will be annotated to be in a specific color class of the resulting bipartition. In each branch, we recursively call the algorithm for every child
t^{\prime}
and take the union of their results. Since
0pt_{T}(t^{\prime})<0pt_{T}(t)
, we can assume that these recursive calls are correct by the induction hypothesis. We make the following claims.

Claim 5.12
.

If
X_{i}\neq\bot
for some
i\in\{1,2,X\}
, then
X_{i}
satisfies the output requirements.

Proof.

We show the argument for
i=1
. Note that the set of leaves of
T_{t}
is the union of the set of leaves of
T_{t^{\prime}}
for
t^{\prime}\in U_{t}
, where
U_{t}
is the set of children of
t
. Therefore the second requirement is satisfied by
X_{1}
. Clearly
X_{1}\subseteq V_{t}
. Let
X_{t}^{\prime}
be the odd cycle transversal of
G[V_{t^{\prime}}\cup S_{1}\cup\chi(t)\cup S_{2}]
for each child
t^{\prime}
of
t
, and let
c_{t^{\prime}}
be a 2-coloring of
G[V_{t^{\prime}}\cup S_{1}\cup\chi(t)\cup S_{2}]-X_{t^{\prime}}
that colors
S_{1}\cup\chi(t)
with color
1
and
S_{2}
with color
2
. We construct a 2-coloring
c_{t}
of
G[V_{t}\cup S_{1}\cup S_{2}]-X_{1}
. For
v\in V_{t}\cup S_{1}\cup S_{2}
let
c_{t}(v)=c_{t^{\prime}}(v)
if
v\in V_{t^{\prime}}
,
c_{t}(v)=1
if
v\in S_{1}\cup\chi(t)
, and
c_{t}(v)=2
if
v\in S_{2}
. Since any edge in
E(G)
has an ancestor-descendant relationship in
T
, it follows that
c_{t}
is a proper 2-coloring of
G[V_{t}\cup S_{1}\cup S_{2}]-X_{1}
. Therefore,
X_{1}
is an odd cycle transversal. Note that the first requirement is also satisfied, since
c_{t}
has
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
in opposite sides of the bipartition. The reasoning for
X_{2}
is symmetric. For
X_{X}
, the only difference is that the unique vertex in
\chi(t)
is removed instead of colored. The claim follows.
∎

From the above claim we have that
|Y_{t}|\leq|X_{i}|
in the case that
X_{i}\neq\bot
for
i\in\{1,2,X\}
.

Claim 5.13
.

If
Y_{t}\neq\bot
is a solution that satisfies the output requirements, then
X_{i}\neq\bot
and
|X_{i}|\leq|Y_{t}|
for some
i\in\{1,2,X\}
.

Proof.

Let
v_{t}
be the unique vertex in
\chi(t)
. Either
v_{t}\in Y_{t}
, or
v_{t}
should be in one of two color classes of the remaining bipartite graph.
Let
c
be a 2-coloring of
G[V_{t}\cup S_{1}\cup S_{2}]-Y_{t}
that has
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
colored 1 and 2 respectively. First consider the case that
v_{t}\notin Y_{t}
and
c(v_{t})=1
. We argue that for each child
t^{\prime}\in U_{t}
, the set
Y_{t}\cap V_{t^{\prime}}
is some solution to the call
\textsc{oct}(G,t^{\prime},(T,\chi),(S_{1}\cup\chi(t),S_{2},S_{X}))
. Since each leaf in
T_{t^{\prime}}
is also a leaf in
T_{t}
, the second requirement is satisfied by
Y_{t}\cap V_{t^{\prime}}
. Clearly
Y_{t}\cap V_{t^{\prime}}
is an odd cycle transversal of
G^{\prime}=G[V_{t^{\prime}}\cup S_{1}\cup\chi(t)\cup S_{2}]
such that
S_{1}\cup\chi(t)
and
S_{2}
are in opposite sides of a bipartition; the coloring
c
restricted to
G^{\prime}
certifies this. Since
Y_{t}\cap V_{t^{\prime}}
is some solution for
t^{\prime}
, an optimal solution for
t^{\prime}
is not larger, so
by induction it follows that the output
X_{t^{\prime}}
of the recursive call satisfies
|X_{t^{\prime}}|\leq|Y_{t}\cap V_{t^{\prime}}|
. By observing that
Y_{t}=\bigcup_{t^{\prime}\in U_{t}}Y_{t}\cap V_{t^{\prime}}
, we get that
X_{1}=\bigcup_{t^{\prime}\in U_{t}}X_{t^{\prime}}
satisfies
|X_{1}|\leq|Y_{t}|
.

The argument for
v_{t}\notin Y_{t}
and
c(v_{t})=2
is symmetric. The argument for
v_{t}\in Y_{t}
is similar; here
Y_{t}=\{v_{t}\}\cup\bigcup_{t^{\prime}\in U_{t}}Y_{t}\cap V_{t^{\prime}}
and therefore
X_{X}=\{v_{t}\}\cup\bigcup_{t^{\prime}\in U_{t}}X_{t^{\prime}}
satisfies
|X_{X}|\leq|Y_{t}|
.
∎

From the two claims above the correctness follows. For
x\in V(T)
, let
n_{x}
and
m_{x}
denote the number of vertices and edges of
G[V_{x}]
, respectively. Recall that
d
is the depth of the overall elimination forest
(F,\chi)
, so that
0pt(T_{t}),0pt_{T}(t)\leq d
for all
t\in V(T)
. To help bound the running time, we use the number of edges in the subtree
T_{t}
as an additional measure of complexity.

Claim 5.14
.

There is a constant
\alpha
such that
oct
(G,t,(T,\chi),(S_{1},S_{2},S_{X}))
can be implemented to run in time bounded by
\alpha\cdot 3^{0pt(T_{t})}\cdot d^{2}\cdot(n_{t}+m_{t}+|E(T_{t})|)
.

Proof.

Proof by induction on
0pt(T_{t})
.

First consider the case that
0pt(T_{t})=0
. Note that
G[\chi(t)]
is a base component of
T
and therefore
G[\chi(t)]
is bipartite. To verify whether
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
are independent sets, we need to be able to efficiently test whether one vertex is a neighbor of another. This would be trivial using an adjacency-matrix representation of the graph, but it would require quadratic space. Instead, we enrich the data structure storing the
\mathsf{bip}
-elimination forest
(F,\chi)
to allow efficient adjacency tests. This enrichment step is performed before the first call to
oct
. With each internal node
t
of
F
, which represents a single vertex
\{v\}=\chi(t)
, we will store a linked list of those edges which connect
v
to a vertex stored in the bag of an ancestor of
t
. Note that, by definition, this list has length at most
0pt_{F}(t)
. It is easy to see that this enrichment step can be done in time
\mathcal{O}(n+m)
.

Using this additional information, we can verify whether
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
are independent sets in time
\mathcal{O}(|S|^{2})=\mathcal{O}(d^{2})
. For each
i\in[2]
, for each vertex
s
of
S_{i}
(stored in the bag
\chi(t^{\prime})=\{s\}
of an ancestor
t^{\prime}
of
t
), iterate through the list of edges connecting
s
to an ancestor. For each of those edges
ss^{\prime}
in the list, we can determine whether
s^{\prime}\in S_{i}
, by setting a local flag in all vertex objects for
S_{i}
to indicate their membership. As each list has length at most
d
, we can test in
\mathcal{O}(|S|\cdot d)=\mathcal{O}(d^{2})
time whether
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
are indeed independent. If so, we solve an
abc
instance. By Lemma
5.10
this takes
\mathcal{O}(|S|(n_{t}+m_{t}))=\mathcal{O}(d(n_{t}+m_{t}))
time. Hence there exists a constant
\alpha
such that the base case is handled in
\alpha\cdot 3^{0pt(T_{t})}\cdot d^{2}\cdot(n_{t}+m_{t}+|E(T_{t})|)
time.

Next consider the case that
0pt(T_{t})>0
. We branch on three instances with the unique vertex in
\chi(t)
added to each of
S_{1}
,
S_{2}
, and
S_{X}
respectively. In each branch, we call the algorithm recursively for each child
t^{\prime}
of
t
. Since
0pt(T_{t^{\prime}})\leq 0pt(T_{t})-1
, by the induction hypothesis each instance can be solved in
\alpha\cdot 3^{0pt(T_{t^{\prime}})}\cdot d^{2}\cdot(n_{t^{\prime}}+m_{t^{\prime}}+|E(T_{t^{\prime}})|)
time. In addition to solving the three recursive calls, the algorithm does some additional work in taking the union of result sets for child subtrees and determining which of these is largest. By storing solutions in linked lists, the union can be computed in time linear in the number of children by concatenating the lists. Hence the additional work that has to be done on top of the recursive calls, can be handled in time
\mathcal{O}(|U_{t}|)
, linear in the number of children. Hence if
\alpha
is sufficiently large, the additional work can be done in time
\alpha\cdot|U_{t}|
. Note that the edges from
t
to its children are present in
T_{t}
but not in the subtrees of its children, so that
|E(T_{t})|=|U_{t}|+\sum_{t^{\prime}\in U_{t}}|E(T_{t^{\prime}})|
. We can now bound the total running time
N
as follows:

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\cdot|U_{t}|+3\cdot\sum_{t^{\prime}\in U_{t}}\alpha\cdot 3^{0pt(T_{t^{\prime}})}\cdot d^{2}\cdot(n_{t^{\prime}}+m_{t^{\prime}}+|E(T_{t^{\prime}})|)

By induction

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\cdot|U_{t}|+3\cdot\alpha\cdot d^{2}\cdot\sum_{t^{\prime}\in U_{t}}3^{0pt(T_{t})-1}\cdot(n_{t^{\prime}}+m_{t^{\prime}}+|E(T_{t^{\prime}})|)

t^{\prime}
is a child of
t

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\cdot|U_{t}|+\alpha\cdot 3^{0pt(T_{t})}\cdot d^{2}\cdot\sum_{t^{\prime}\in U_{t}}\cdot(n_{t^{\prime}}+m_{t^{\prime}}+|E(T_{t^{\prime}})|)

Rewriting

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\cdot|U_{t}|+\alpha\cdot 3^{0pt(T_{t})}\cdot d^{2}\cdot(n_{t}+m_{t}+|E(T_{t})|-|U_{t}|)

Bound on
|E(T_{t})|

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\cdot|U_{t}|+\left(\alpha\cdot 3^{0pt(T_{t})}\cdot d^{2}\cdot(n_{t}+m_{t}+|E(T_{t})|)\right)-\alpha\cdot|U_{t}|)

Rewriting

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\cdot 3^{0pt(T_{t})}\cdot d^{2}\cdot(n_{t}+m_{t}+|E(T_{t})|)

Canceling terms

This proves the claim.
∎

Since we initially call the algorithm on each tree of the elimination forest, which we can compute in linear time, the result follows. To see the polynomial space guarantee, note that the recursion tree has depth at most
d+1
, and for each instance we store a single node
t\in V(T)
, annotations
(S_{1},S_{2},S_{X})
of size
\mathcal{O}(d)
, and construct three odd cycle transversals
X_{X}
,
X_{1}
, and
X_{2}
of
\mathcal{O}(n)
vertices. Furthermore the call to
abc
runs in polynomial time and therefore uses polynomial space too.
∎

In the algorithm above we assumed we were given some decomposition. Since we have seen that we can compute approximate
\mathsf{bip}
-elimination forests in FPT time, we have the following.

Corollary 5.15
.

Odd cycle transversal
is FPT parameterized by
k=\mathbf{ed}_{\mathsf{bip}}(G)
, furthermore it is solvable in
n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}+2^{\mathcal{O}(k^{3}\log k)}\cdot(n+m)
time and polynomial space.

Proof.

Let
k=\mathbf{ed}_{\mathsf{bip}}(G)
. By
4.35
, we can compute a
\mathsf{bip}
-elimination forest of depth
\mathcal{O}(k^{3}\log k)
in polynomial time and space. The result follows by supplying this elimination forest to Theorem
5.11
.
∎

We remark that this running time can be slightly improved by using the other result from
4.35
as we could compute a
\mathsf{bip}
-elimination forest of depth
\mathcal{O}(k^{2})
, but then we lose the polynomial space guarantee.

Given a tree
\mathsf{bip}
-decomposition

Fiorini et al.
[
44
]
give an explicit algorithm for computing the size of a minimum odd cycle transversal in a tree decomposition of width
\mathbf{tw}
in time
\mathcal{O}(3^{3\mathbf{tw}}\cdot\mathbf{tw}\cdot n)
. We follow their algorithm to show that a similar strategy works when given a tree
\mathsf{bip}
-decomposition. Most of the reasoning overlaps with
[
44
]
, we include some of it for completeness.

Theorem 5.16
.

Odd cycle traversal
can be solved in time
3^{3k}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
when given a tree
\mathsf{bip}
-decomposition of width
k-1
consisting of
n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
nodes.

Proof.

First, we apply Lemma
5.5
to turn the given tree
\mathsf{bip}
-decomposition
(T,\chi,L)
into a nice one.
This takes time
\mathcal{O}(n+|V(T)|\cdot k^{2})
and generates a nice tree
\mathsf{bip}
-decomposition of the same width, such that
|V(T)|=\mathcal{O}(kn)
.
In particular, we can assume that
T
is a binary tree.

Let
t^{*}\in V(T)
be the root of
T
. Furthermore, for
t\in V(T)
, let
T_{t}
be the subtree of
T
rooted at
t
. For each
e=uv\in E(G)
, assign a specific node
t(e)\in V(T)
, such that
u,v\in\chi(t)
. Let
E_{t}
be the set of edges between the vertices in
\chi(t)
for
t\in V(T)
. Let us define graph
G(t)
with vertex set
\chi(t)
and edge set
E_{t}
, and the graph
G(T_{t})
with vertex set
\bigcup_{t^{\prime}\in T_{t}}\chi(t^{\prime})
and edge set
\bigcup_{t^{\prime}\in T_{t}}E_{t^{\prime}}
. We associate with
t\in V(T)
a set
\mathcal{A}_{t}
of ordered triples
\Pi_{t}=(L_{t},R_{t},W_{t})
, which forms a 3-partition of
\chi(t)\setminus L
. Note that
|\mathcal{A}_{t}|
is at most
3^{k}
.

The algorithm works from the leaves up, and for each partition
\Pi_{t}
stores the size of a minimum odd cycle transversal
\hat{W}_{t}
in
G(T_{t})
such that
W_{t}\subseteq\hat{W}_{t}
and
L_{t}
and
R_{t}
are in opposite color classes of
G(T_{t})-\hat{W}_{t}
. This value is stored in
f(\Pi)
, and is infinity if no such transversal exists. For a non-leaf
t
with a child
r
, a partition of
\Pi_{r}
is said to be consistent with
\Pi_{t}
, denoted
\Pi_{r}\sim\Pi_{t}
, if
W_{t}\cap V(S_{r})\subseteq W_{r}
,
L_{t}\cap V(S_{r})\subseteq L_{r}
, and
R_{t}\cap V(S_{r})\subseteq R_{r}
.
Let
r,s
be children of
t
; if there is only one child, we omit the terms for
s
.
If both
L_{t}
and
R_{t}
are independent sets in
G(t)
, we have

f(\Pi_{t})=\min_{\Pi_{r}\sim\Pi_{t},\Pi_{s}\sim\Pi_{t}}f(\Pi_{r})+f(\Pi_{s})+|W_{t}-(W_{r}\cup W_{s})|-|W_{r}\cap W_{s}|

Otherwise, set
f(\Pi_{t})=\infty
. For a leaf
t\in V(T)
, we set

f(\Pi_{t})=|W_{t}|+\textsc{abc}(G[\chi(t)\cap L],N(L_{t})\cap\chi(t),N(R_{t})\cap\chi(t),n)

Since
\chi(t)\cap L=\emptyset
for non-leaf nodes
t\in V(T)
, the correctness follows as in
[
44
]
. In each leaf node, similarly as in Theorem
5.11
,
we obtain an instance of
Annotated bipartite coloring
(here with budget
n
), which can be solved in polynomial time (Lemma
5.10
).

The total work in the internal nodes remains
\mathcal{O}(k\cdot 3^{3k}\cdot n)
as in
[
44
]
. The total work in the leaves takes
3^{k}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
time.
∎

We note that the algorithm of Fiorini et al.
[
44
]
we adapted above does not have the best possible dependency on treewidth. The dependency on
\mathbf{tw}_{\mathsf{bip}}
of our algorithm can be improved by exploiting more properties of nice tree
\mathcal{H}
-decompositions, to improve the exponential factor to
3^{k}
. Similarly, the polynomial in the running time can be improved to
k^{\mathcal{O}(1)}\cdot(n+m)
. Two additional ideas are needed to achieve this speed-up. First of all, the computations for
Annotated bipartite coloring
in the leaf nodes can be cut off. By Corollary
5.2
, an optimal solution contains at most
k+1
vertices from any base component of a
\mathsf{bip}
-decomposition of width
k
. Hence it suffices to set the budget for the
abc
instance to
k+1
, and assign a table cell the value
\infty
if no solution was found. While this causes some entries in the table to become
\infty
, the optimum solution and final answer are preserved. The second additional idea that is needed is an efficient data structure for testing adjacencies in graphs of bounded
\mathsf{bip}
-treewidth, similarly as explained in
[
11
]
. We chose to present a simpler yet slower algorithm for ease of readability.

Since we can compute tree
\mathsf{bip}
-decompositions approximately, we conclude with the following.

Corollary 5.17
.

The
Odd cycle transversal
problem can be solved in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(k^{3})}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
when parameterized by
k=\mathbf{tw}_{\mathsf{bip}}(G)
.

Proof.

By
4.35
we can compute a tree
\mathsf{bip}
-decomposition of width
\mathcal{O}(k^{3})
in
2^{\mathcal{O}(k\log k)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
time. The result follows by supplying this decomposition to Theorem
5.16
. The algorithm that computes the minimum size of an odd cycle transversal can be turned into an algorithm that constructs a minimum solution by standard techniques or self-reduction, in the same asymptotic time bounds.
∎

5.2.2
Vertex cover

In this section we give algorithms for the
Vertex cover
problem when given a decomposition with base components of some hereditary graph class
\mathcal{H}
in which the problem is polynomial-time solvable. In the
Vertex cover
problem, we are given a graph
G
, and ask for the (size of a) minimum vertex set
S\subseteq V(G)
such that for each
uv\in E(G)
,
S\cap\{u,v\}\neq\emptyset
. Such a set
S
is called a vertex cover.

Note that the
Vertex cover
problem can be stated as hitting forbidden induced subgraph, namely
K_{2}
, which we will treat in Section
5.3.4
.
However for the special case of
Vertex cover
, we are able to handle the problem for a broader spectrum of parameterizations.

Given an
\mathcal{H}
-elimination forest

We present a branching algorithm for
Vertex cover
in Algorithm
2
that is similar to Algorithm
1
for
Odd cycle transversal
. Again we branch on the top-most vertex of an elimination forest. In the case of
Vertex cover
a vertex is either in the vertex cover (and added to
S_{I}
) or outside the vertex cover (and added to
S_{O}
). The intuition for the computation in the base components is as follows. For any ancestor that was chosen to not be in the vertex cover, any neighbor contained in the base component must be in the vertex cover. Since
\mathcal{H}
is hereditary, the base component where these vertices are deleted still belongs to
\mathcal{H}
. Therefore we can run an algorithm on the remaining graph that solves vertex cover in polynomial time on graphs from
\mathcal{H}
. Let
\textsc{A}_{\mathcal{H}}
be a polynomial-time algorithm that finds a minimum vertex cover in a graph
G\in\mathcal{H}
.

Algorithm 2

vc
(Graph
G
,
t\in V(T)
,
\mathcal{H}
-elimination forest
(T,\chi)
,
(S_{I},S_{O})
)

1:

2:

(T,\chi)
is an
\mathcal{H}
-elimination forest of
G
that consists of a single tree.

3:

S=S_{I}\cup S_{O}=\{\chi(t^{\prime})\mid t^{\prime}\text{ is a proper ancestor of }t\}
.

4:

S_{I}
and
S_{O}
are disjoint.

5:

6:

A vertex cover
X_{t}\subseteq V_{t}
of
G[V_{t}\cup S_{O}]
of minimum size. If no such
X_{t}
exists return
\bot
.

7:

if

t
is a leaf
then

8:

if

\exists_{u,w\in S_{O}}uw\in E(G)

then

9:

return

\bot

10:

else

11:

Use
\textsc{A}_{\mathcal{H}}
to compute a minimum vertex cover
X
of
G[\chi(t)\setminus N_{G}(S_{O})]

12:

return

X\cup(N_{G}(S_{O})\cap\chi(t))

13:

else

14:

Let
U_{t}
be the children of
t
.

15:

Let
X_{I}=\chi(t)\cup\bigcup_{t^{\prime}\in U_{t}}\textsc{vc}(G,t^{\prime},(T,\chi),(S_{I}\cup\chi(t),S_{O}))
.

16:

Let
X_{O}=\bigcup_{t^{\prime}\in U_{t}}\textsc{vc}(G,t^{\prime},(T,\chi),(S_{I},S_{O}\cup\chi(t)))
.

17:

return
smallest of
X_{I}
and
X_{O}
that is not
\bot
, or
\bot
otherwise.

We note that the branching can be improved using the following idea. Whenever we put some vertex
v
in
S_{O}
(so
v
is not part of the cover), then we must have all of
N_{G}(v)
in the vertex cover. We do not include it here for ease of presentation.

Theorem 5.18
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a hereditary graph class in which
Vertex cover
is polynomial time solvable. Then given an
\mathcal{H}
-elimination forest
(F,\chi)
with depth
d
,
Vertex cover
is solvable in
2^{d}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
time and polynomial space.

Proof.

We solve
Vertex cover
by summing the results of
vc
(G[V_{r_{T}}],r_{T},(T,\chi_{T}),(\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset))
over all trees
T
in the forest
F
. Here
r_{T}\in V(T)
is the root of
T
, and
\chi_{T}
is the function
\chi
restricted to
V(T)
. We argue the correctness of Algorithm
2
by induction on
0pt(T_{t})
. Consider a call
vc
(G,t,(T,\chi),(S_{I},S_{O}))
that satisfies the input requirements.

If
0pt(T_{t})=0
, then
t
is a leaf and
G[\chi(t)]\in\mathcal{H}
. For any set
Y_{t}\subseteq V_{t}
that satisfies the output requirements, the set
S_{O}
must be an independent set. If this is not the case, we correctly return
\bot
. Otherwise for
Y_{t}
to be a vertex cover, every vertex from
N_{G}(S_{O})\cap\chi(t)
must be in the vertex cover. Therefore we return the union of
N_{G}(S_{O})\cap\chi(t)
and the result of
\textsc{A}_{\mathcal{H}}(G[\chi(t)\setminus N_{G}(S_{O})])
.

Otherwise, if
0pt(T_{t})>0
, then
t
is not a leaf. We branch on the unique vertex in
\chi(t)
to be either in the vertex cover or not. In each branch, we recursively call the algorithm for each child
t^{\prime}
of
t
. Since
0pt(T_{t^{\prime}})\leq 0pt(T_{t})-1
, by the induction hypothesis these recursive calls are correct. Since there is an ancestor descendant relation for every edge in
E(G)
, the union of vertex covers for in the recursively called subinstances form a vertex cover for the current instance. Since this branching is exhaustive, an optimal vertex cover is found in one of the branches if it exists. If
X_{I}=X_{O}=\bot
, then we can return
\infty
for the current instance as well.

Finally, we bound the running time and space requirements, by analyzing the recursion tree generated by the initial calls on each tree
T
of the forest
F
. For each node
t\in V(T)
, there are exactly
2^{0pt_{T}(t)}
recursive calls to Algorithm
2
with
t
as the second parameter, one for every partition of the nodes in the bags of ancestors of
t
into
S_{I}
and
S_{O}
. Within one call, we either verify that
S_{O}
is an independent set in
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
time and call
\textsc{A}_{\mathcal{H}}
that runs in
n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
time, or compute the union of the partial results given by other calls in
n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
time. Hence the total size of the recursion tree is bounded by
2^{d}\cdot|V(F)|
and the work per call is polynomial in
n
. As we store a polynomial amount of information in each call, this yields the desired bounds on running time and space requirements.
∎

In the algorithm above we assumed we are given some
\mathcal{H}
-elimination forest. One example where
Vertex cover
is polynomial time solvable, but no
\mathcal{H}
-elimination forest can be computed in FPT time is the class of perfect graphs, which we prove in Theorem
6.3
. For some graph classes
\mathcal{H}
we do know how to compute such decompositions, which gives the following.

Corollary 5.19
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a graph class such that
\mathcal{H}\in\{\text{bipartite, chordal, interval}\}
or
\mathcal{H}
is characterized by a finite family of forbidden induced subgraphs and
Vertex cover
is polynomial-time solvable on
\mathcal{H}
. Then
Vertex cover
is FPT parameterized by
\mathbf{ed}_{\mathcal{H}}(G)
. Furthermore it can be solved in
2^{\mathbf{ed}_{\mathsf{\mathcal{H}}}(G)^{\mathcal{O}(1)}}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
time using polynomial space.

Proof.

It is well known that
Vertex cover
is polynomial time solvable on bipartite, chordal, and interval graphs, as these are subclasses of perfect graphs
[
58
]
. Let
k=\mathbf{ed}_{\mathcal{H}}(G)
. By
4.35
, we can compute an
\mathcal{H}
-elimination forest of depth
k^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
in
2^{k^{\mathcal{O}(1)}}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
time
and polynomial space, for each considered class
\mathcal{H}
. The result follows by supplying the computed decomposition to Theorem
5.18
.
∎

One example of a hereditary graph class
\mathcal{H}
that satisfies the premises of the theorem is the class of claw-free (induced
K_{1,3}
-free) graphs. A maximum independent set (and therefore, its complement which forms a minimum vertex cover) can be found in polynomial-time in claw-free graphs
[
87
,
104
]
.

Taking the idea of relaxed parameterizations for
Vertex cover
one step further, one could even combine multiple graph classes in which the problem can efficiently be solved. For example, if
\mathcal{H}
is the class of graphs where each connected component is bipartite, or chordal, or claw-free, then
Vertex cover
is still FPT parameterized by
\mathbf{ed}_{\mathcal{H}}
. It is easy to see that the decomposition algorithms can be adapted to work with the combination of these graph classes, while Theorem
5.18
directly applies. This leads to even smaller parameter values. Using the
\mathcal{H}
-treewidth counterpart (Theorem
5.20
), we also obtain fixed-parameter tractability for the parameterization by
\mathbf{tw}_{\mathcal{H}}
.

Given a tree
\mathcal{H}
-decomposition

Niedermeier
[
89
]
gives an algorithm to solve
Vertex cover
given a tree decomposition of width
d-1
in time
2^{d}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
using a standard dynamic programming approach. We adapt this algorithm to work with
\mathcal{H}
-decompositions for hereditary graph classes
\mathcal{H}
in which vertex cover is polynomial time solvable similar to the
\mathcal{H}
-elimination forest case.

Theorem 5.20
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a hereditary graph class on which
Vertex cover
is polynomial-time solvable.
Vertex cover
can be solved in time
2^{k}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
when given a tree
\mathcal{H}
-decomposition of width
k-1
consisting of
n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
nodes.

Proof.

We adapt the algorithm by Niedermeier
[
89
, Thm. 10.14]
. Choose an arbitrary root for the given tree
\mathcal{H}
-decomposition
(T,\chi,L)
. For
t\in V(T)
, let
T_{t}
be the subtree rooted at
t
. We keep a table for every
t\in V(T)
, with a row for every assignment
C_{t}\colon\chi(t)\setminus L\to\{0,1\}
. For every such assignment, we store a value
m_{t}(C_{t})
that denotes the number of vertices in a minimum vertex cover
V^{\prime}
of the graph induced by
\bigcup_{t^{\prime}\in T_{t}}\chi(t^{\prime})
such that
V^{\prime}\cap(\chi(t)\setminus L)=C_{t}^{-1}(1)
. That is, we consider solutions which contain all vertices in
C_{t}^{-1}(1)
and none of
C_{t}^{-1}(0)
. Not every assignment leads to a solution:
m_{t}(C_{t})=\infty
if there is some edge
uv
with
C_{t}(u)=C_{t}(v)=0
.

We only have to adapt the computation for the leaf nodes
t
of the decomposition; the update steps remain identical as before
[
89
]
since
\chi(t)\cap L=\emptyset
for non-leaf nodes
t\in V(T)
.

For a leaf node
t
and assignment
C_{t}
, define the auxiliary graph
G^{\prime}:=G[(\chi(t)\cap L)\setminus N_{G}(C_{t}^{-1}(0))]
corresponding to the base component without the vertices which are forced to be in the solution to cover edges incident on
C_{t}^{-1}(0)
. As the solutions we are asking for contain all of
C_{t}^{-1}(1)
, none of
C_{t}^{-1}(0)
and therefore all of
N_{G}(C_{t}^{-1}(0))\cap(\chi(t)\cap L)
, and furthermore have to cover all edges of
G^{\prime}
, the value of
m_{t}
can be computed as follows:

m_{t}(C_{t})=\begin{cases}+\infty&\mbox{if $\exists uv\in E(G)\colon u,v\in C_{t}^{-1}(0)$}\\
|C_{t}^{-1}(1)|+|N_{G}(C_{t}^{-1}(0))\cap\chi(t)\cap L|+\mathsf{minvc}(G^{\prime})&\mbox{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Note that since
\mathcal{H}
is hereditary and
G[\chi(t)\cap L]\in\mathcal{H}
, it follows that
G^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}
. Therefore we can compute the size
\mathsf{minvc}(G^{\prime})
of a minimum vertex cover in
G^{\prime}
in
n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
time.
The running time of
2^{k}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
follows.
∎

As before, for certain
\mathcal{H}
we can compute tree
\mathcal{H}
-decompositions to arrive at fixed parameter tractability results.

Corollary 5.21
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a graph class such that
\mathcal{H}\in\{\text{bipartite, chordal, interval}\}
or
\mathcal{H}
is characterized by a finite family of forbidden induced subgraphs and
Vertex cover
is polynomial-time solvable on
\mathcal{H}
. Then
Vertex cover
is FPT parameterized by
\mathbf{tw}_{\mathcal{H}}(G)
and can be solved in
2^{\mathbf{tw}_{\mathsf{\mathcal{H}}}(G)^{\mathcal{O}(1)}}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
time.

Proof.

It is well known that
Vertex cover
is polynomial time solvable on bipartite, chordal, and interval graphs, as these are subclasses of perfect graphs
[
58
]
. Let
k=\mathbf{tw}_{\mathcal{H}}(G)
.
By
4.35
, we can compute a tree
\mathcal{H}
-decomposition of width
k^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
in time
2^{k^{\mathcal{O}(1)}}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
, for each considered class
\mathcal{H}
.
The result follows by supplying the computed decomposition to Theorem
5.20
.
∎

5.2.3
Hitting forbidden cliques

We say that a graph is
K_{\ell}
-free if it does not contain
K_{\ell}
as a subgraph, or equivalently, as an induced subgraph. In this section we show how to obtain an FPT algorithm for the
K_{\ell}
-free deletion
problem where one is given an (
K_{\ell}
-free)-elimination forest, for some
\ell\geq 2
,
and the goal is to compute the (size of a) minimum vertex set
S\subseteq V(G)
such that
G-S
is
K_{\ell}
-free. Here
\ell
is assumed to be a constant. Note that the
Vertex cover
problem is equivalent to
K_{2}
-free deletion
.

The fact that
K_{\ell}
-free deletion
problem has an FPT algorithm parameterized by the depth of an (
K_{\ell}
-free)-elimination forest will follow from the more general Theorem
5.48
on hitting forbidden induced subgraphs, but the branching algorithm we present here has the additional feature that it runs in polynomial space.
It is similar to Algorithm
2
for
Vertex cover
, where we branch on the top-most vertex of an elimination forest. As with
Vertex cover
, a vertex is either in the solution (and added to
S_{I}
), or not in the solution (and added to
S_{O}
). The key idea is that a clique can occur only on a single root-to-leaf path in an elimination forest.
The base component might have some ancestors in
S_{O}
that can still be part of some
K_{\ell}
. In the case of
\ell=2
(
Vertex cover
), there is only one remaining option to hit each
K_{2}
, namely to remove each neighbor of
S_{O}
in the base component. In general there are can be as many as
\ell-1
options to consider.
Since the base component is
K_{\ell}
-free, by Corollary
5.2
it follows that any optimal solution uses at most
d
vertices from the base component, where
d
is the depth of the (
K_{\ell}
-free)-elimination forest.

Consider the
K_{\ell}
-free deletion with forbidden vertices
(
K_{\ell}
-fdfv
) problem, where we are given a graph
G
, a set of undeletable vertices
S\subseteq V(G)
, and integer
k
, whereas
\ell
is treated as a constant. The task is to output a minimum set
X\subseteq V(G)\setminus S
such that
G-X
has no
K_{\ell}
as induced subgraph and
|X|\leq k
, or
\bot
if no such set exists. We can solve this problem by finding an induced
K_{\ell}
in
\mathcal{O}(n^{\ell})
time, and then branching on the at most
\ell
ways to delete a non-forbidden vertex to hit this subgraph. If a solution has not been found after branching for
k
levels deep, return
\bot
. Hence we get the following observation; a formal proof follows from the more general algorithm of Lemma
5.47
which is presented later.

Observation 5.22
.

K_{\ell}
-fdfv
(G,S,k)
is solvable in
\ell^{k}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
time and polynomial space.

Algorithm 3

K_{\ell}
-free deletion
(Graph
G
,
t\in V(T)
,
(T,\chi)
,
(S_{I},S_{O})
)

1:

2:

(T,\chi)
is a (
K_{\ell}
-free)-elimination forest
G
that consists of a single tree.

3:

S=S_{I}\cup S_{O}=\{\chi(t^{\prime})\mid t^{\prime}\text{ is a proper ancestor of }t\}
.

4:

S_{I}
and
S_{O}
are disjoint.

5:

6:

A
K_{\ell}
-free deletion set
X_{t}\subseteq V_{t}
of
G[V_{t}\cup S_{O}]
of minimum size, such that
|X_{t}\cap\chi(t^{\prime})|\leq 0pt_{T}(t^{\prime})
for each leaf
t^{\prime}\in V(T_{t})
. If no such
X_{t}
exists return
\bot
.

7:

if

t
is a leaf
then

8:

if

G[S_{O}]
contains
K_{\ell}
as induced subgraph
then

9:

return

\bot

10:

else

11:

return

K_{\ell}
-fdfv
(
G[\chi(t)\cup S_{O}],S_{O},0pt_{T}(t))

12:

else

13:

Let
U_{t}
be the children of
t
.

14:

Let
X_{I}=\chi(t)\cup\bigcup_{t^{\prime}\in U_{t}}\textsc{$K_{\ell}$-free deletion}(G,t^{\prime},(T,\chi),(S_{I}\cup\chi(t),S_{O}))
.

15:

Let
X_{O}=\bigcup_{t^{\prime}\in U_{t}}\textsc{$K_{\ell}$-free deletion}(G,t^{\prime},(T,\chi),(S_{I},S_{O}\cup\chi(t)))
.

16:

return
smallest of
X_{I}
and
X_{O}
that is not
\bot
, or
\bot
otherwise.

Theorem 5.23
.

Given a (
K_{\ell}
-free)-elimination forest
(F,\chi)
with depth
d
for some constant
\ell\geq 2
,
K_{\ell}
-free deletion
is solvable in
(2\ell)^{d}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
time and polynomial space.

Proof.

We solve
K_{\ell}
-free deletion
by summing the results of
K_{\ell}
-free deletion
(G[V_{r_{T}}],r_{T},\allowbreak(T,\chi_{T}),(\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset))
over all trees
T
in the forest
F
. Here
r_{T}\in V(T)
is the root of
T
, and
\chi_{T}
is the function
\chi
restricted to
V(T)
. To see that this correctly solves the problem, note that any optimal solution takes at most
0pt_{T}(t^{\prime})
vertices from
\chi(t^{\prime})
for every leaf
t^{\prime}\in V(T)
by Corollary
5.2
.
We argue the correctness of Algorithm
3
by induction on
0pt(T_{t})
. Consider a call
K_{\ell}
-free deletion
(G,t,(T,\chi),(S_{I},S_{O}))
that satisfies the input requirements.

If
0pt(T_{t})=0
, then
t
is a leaf and
G[\chi(t)\cap L]
is
K_{\ell}
-free.
If graph
G[S_{O}]
is not
K_{\ell}
-free,
then there is no
X_{t}\subseteq V_{t}
that satisfies the output requirements.
Otherwise, the problem is equivalent to an instance of
K_{\ell}
-free deletion with forbidden vertices
for arguments
(G[\chi(t)\cup S_{O}],S_{O},0pt_{T}(t))
.

In the remaining case
0pt(T_{t})>0
, so
t
is not a leaf. Recall that there is an ancestor-descendant relation for every edge in
E(G)
. Therefore any clique occurs on a single root-to-leaf path in the decomposition. We branch on the unique vertex in
\chi(t)
to be either in the solution or not, and recursively call the algorithm for each child
t^{\prime}
of
t
. Since
0pt(T_{t^{\prime}})\leq 0pt(T_{t})-1
, these calls are correct by the induction hypothesis. Since a clique occurs on a single root to leaf path, the union of solutions for the recursively called subinstances forms a solution for the current instance. Since this branching is exhaustive, the minimum size solution occurs on one of them if it exists. If all subinstances return
\bot
, then we can return
\bot
for the current instance as well.

Finally, we bound the running time and space requirements, by analyzing the recursion tree generated by the initial calls on each tree
T
of the forest
F
. For each node
t\in V(T)
, there are exactly
2^{0pt_{T}(t)}
recursive calls to Algorithm
2
with
t
as the second parameter, one for every partition of the nodes in the bags of ancestors of
t
into
S_{I}
and
S_{O}
. Within one call, we either verify that
G[S_{O}]
does not contain
K_{\ell}
as induced subgraph in
n^{\mathcal{O}(\ell)}=n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
time and call
K_{\ell}
-
fdfv
that runs in
\ell^{0pt_{T}(t)}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
time, or compute the union of the partial results given by other calls in
n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
time. Hence the total size of the recursion tree is bounded by
2^{d}\cdot|V(F)|
and the work per call takes at most
\ell^{d}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
time. Note that
K_{\ell}
-
fdfv
can be solved using polynomial space. Therefore, as we store a polynomial amount of information in each call, this yields the desired bounds on running time and space requirements.
∎

Corollary 5.24
.

For each fixed
\ell\geq 2
,
K_{\ell}
-free deletion
is FPT parameterized by
k=\mathbf{ed}_{\mathsf{\text{$K_{\ell}$-free}}}(G)
. Furthermore it can be solved in
2^{\mathcal{O}(k^{3}\log k)}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
time using polynomial space.

Proof.

Let
k=\mathbf{ed}_{\mathsf{\text{$K_{\ell}$-free}}}(G)
. By Theorem
4.35
, we can compute a (
K_{\ell}
-free)-elimination forest of depth
\mathcal{O}(k^{3}\log k)
in
2^{\mathcal{O}(k)}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
time and polynomial space. The result follows by supplying this decomposition to Theorem
5.23
.
∎

5.3
Generic algorithms via
A
-exhaustive families

5.3.1
A
-exhaustive families and boundaried graphs

In this section we introduce the main tools needed to present our most general framework for dynamic programming
on tree
\mathcal{H}
-decompositions. We follow the ideas of gluing graphs and finite state property dating back to the results of Fellows and Langston
[
42
]
(cf.
[
5
,
13
]
).

The following definition formalizes a key idea for the dynamic programming routine: to compute a restricted set of partial solutions out of which an optimal solution can always be built.

Definition 5.25
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a graph class, let
G
be a graph, and let
A\subseteq V(G)
. Then we say that a family
\mathcal{S}\subseteq 2^{A}
of subsets of
A
is
A
-exhaustive for
\mathcal{H}
-deletion
on
G
if for every minimum-size set
S\subseteq V(G)
for which
G-S\in\mathcal{H}
, there exists
S_{A}\in\mathcal{S}
such that for
S^{\prime}:=(S\setminus A)\cup S_{A}
we have
|S^{\prime}|\leq|S|
and
G-S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}
.

As a consequence of this definition, if
\mathcal{S}
is
A
-exhaustive for
\mathcal{H}
-deletion
then there exists an optimal solution
S
to
\mathcal{H}
-deletion
with
S\cap A\in\mathcal{S}
.

The notion of
A
-exhaustive families is similar in spirit to that of
q
-representative families
for matroids, which have been used in recent algorithms working on graph decompositions
[
46
,
75
]
(cf.
[
32
, §12.3]
) to trim the set of partial solutions stored by a dynamic program while preserving the existence of an optimal solution. As the desired outcome of the replacement process in our case is not defined in terms of independence in a matroid and there is no particular importance of a given integer
q
, we use different terminology for our concept of exhaustive families.
We reserve the name of a
representative family
to refer to a set of representatives for each equivalence class in the relation introduced in Definition
5.28
.

The following observation shows how
A
-exhaustive families can be extended to supersets
A^{\prime}\supseteq A
by brute force. If
|A^{\prime}\setminus A|
is bounded, the increase in the size of the exhaustive family can be controlled.

Observation 5.26
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a graph class, let
G
be a graph, let
A\subseteq V(G)
and let
\mathcal{S}\subseteq 2^{A}
be
A
-exhaustive for
\mathcal{H}
-deletion
on
G
. Then for any set
A^{\prime}\supseteq A
, the family
\mathcal{S^{\prime}}\subseteq 2^{A^{\prime}}
defined as follows has size at most
|\mathcal{S}|\cdot 2^{|A^{\prime}\setminus A|}
and is
A^{\prime}
-exhaustive for
\mathcal{H}
-deletion
on
G
:

\mathcal{S}^{\prime}:=\{S_{1}\cup S^{*}\mid S_{1}\in\mathcal{S}\wedge S^{*}\subseteq(A^{\prime}\setminus A)\}.

A similar brute-force extension can be done when merging exhaustive families for disjoint subsets
A_{1},A_{2}
into a an
A
-exhaustive family for a common superset
A\supseteq A_{1}\cup A_{2}
. We will use this step to handle a variation of standard
\mathsf{join}
bags in a tree decomposition.

Lemma 5.27
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a graph class and let
G
be a graph. Let
A_{1},A_{2}\subseteq V(G)
be disjoint sets and let
\mathcal{S}_{1},\mathcal{S}_{2}
be
A_{1}
-exhaustive (respectively,
A_{2}
-exhaustive) for
\mathcal{H}
-deletion
on
G
. Then for any set
A^{\prime}\supseteq A_{1}\cup A_{2}
, the family
\mathcal{S^{\prime}}\subseteq 2^{A^{\prime}}
defined as follows has size at most
|\mathcal{S}_{1}|\cdot|\mathcal{S}_{2}|\cdot 2^{|A^{\prime}\setminus(A_{1}\cup A_{2})|}
and is
A^{\prime}
-exhaustive for
\mathcal{H}
-deletion
on
G
:

\mathcal{S}^{\prime}:=\{S_{1}\cup S_{2}\cup S^{*}\mid S_{1}\in\mathcal{S}_{1}\wedge S_{2}\in\mathcal{S}_{2}\wedge S^{*}\subseteq(A^{\prime}\setminus(A_{1}\cup A_{2}))\}.

Proof.

The bound on
|\mathcal{S}^{\prime}|
is clear from the definition. Consider an arbitrary optimal solution
S\subseteq V(G)
to
\mathcal{H}
-deletion
on
G
; we will show there exists
\widehat{S}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
such that
(S\setminus A^{\prime})\cup\widehat{S}
is an optimal solution. We use a two-step argument.

Since
\mathcal{S}_{1}
is
A_{1}
-exhaustive, there exists
S_{1}\in\mathcal{S}_{1}
such that
S^{\prime}:=(S\setminus A_{1})\cup S_{1}
is again an optimal solution.

Applying a similar step to
S^{\prime}
, as
\mathcal{S}_{2}
is
A_{2}
-exhaustive there exists
S_{2}\in\mathcal{S}_{2}
such that
S^{\prime\prime}:=(S^{\prime}\setminus A_{2})\cup S_{2}
is an optimal solution.

Since
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
are disjoint, we have
S^{\prime\prime}\cap A_{1}=S_{1}
and
S^{\prime\prime}\cap A_{2}=S_{2}
. Let
S^{*}:=S^{\prime\prime}\cap(A^{\prime}\setminus(A_{1}\cup A_{2}))
. It follows that the set
\widehat{S}=S_{1}\cup S_{2}\cup S^{*}
belongs to
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
. Now note that
S\setminus A^{\prime}=S^{\prime\prime}\setminus A^{\prime}
as we have only replaced parts of the solution within
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
, while
A^{\prime}\supseteq A_{1}\cup A_{2}
. Hence
(S\setminus A^{\prime})\cup\widehat{S}=S^{\prime\prime}
is an optimal solution, which concludes the proof.
∎

Note that Lemma
5.27
implies Observation
5.26
by letting
A_{2}=\emptyset,\,\mathcal{S}_{2}=\{\emptyset\}
.

Boundaried graphs

For a function
f\colon A\to B
and a set
A^{\prime}\subseteq A
, we denote by
f_{|A^{\prime}}\colon A^{\prime}\to B
the restriction of
f
to
A^{\prime}
.
A
k
-boundaried graph is a triple
\widehat{G}=(G,X,\lambda)
, where
G
is a graph,
X\subseteq V(G)
, and
\lambda\colon[k]\to X
is a bijection.
We define
V(\widehat{G})=V(G)
.
Two
k
-boundaried graphs
(G_{1},X_{1},\lambda_{1})
,
(G_{2},X_{2},\lambda_{2})
are compatible if
\lambda_{2}\circ\lambda_{1}^{-1}
is a graph isomorphism between
G_{1}[X_{1}]
and
G_{2}[X_{2}]
.
Two
k
-boundaried graphs
(G_{1},X_{1},\lambda_{1})
,
(G_{2},X_{2},\lambda_{2})
are isomorphic if there is a graph isomorphism
\pi\colon V(G_{1})\to V(G_{2})
,
such that
\pi_{|X_{1}}=\lambda_{2}\circ\lambda_{1}^{-1}
.
The gluing operation
\oplus
takes two compatible
k
-boundaried graphs
(G_{1},X_{1},\lambda_{1})
,
(G_{2},X_{2},\lambda_{2})
, creates their disjoint
union, and then identifies the vertices
\lambda_{1}[i],\lambda_{2}[i]
for each
i\in[k]
.
A tri-separation
(A,X,B)
of order
k
in graph
G
can be naturally decomposed as
(G[A\cup X],X,\lambda)\oplus(G[B\cup X],X,\lambda)
for an arbitrary bijection
\lambda\colon[|X|]\to X
.

Definition 5.28
.

We say that two
k
-boundaried graphs
(G_{1},X_{1},\lambda_{1})
,
(G_{2},X_{2},\lambda_{2})
are
(\mathcal{H},k)
-equivalent if they are compatible and for every compatible
k
-boundaried graph
(G_{3},X_{3},\lambda_{3})
, it holds that
(G_{1},X_{1},\lambda_{1})\oplus(G_{3},X_{3},\lambda_{3})\in\mathcal{H}\Longleftrightarrow(G_{2},X_{2},\lambda_{2})\oplus(G_{3},X_{3},\lambda_{3})\in\mathcal{H}
.

Observe that if
(G_{1},X_{1},\lambda_{1})
and
(G_{2},X_{2},\lambda_{2})
are compatible and
G_{1},G_{2}
do not belong to
\mathcal{H}
, they are
(\mathcal{H},k)
-equivalent because we only consider hereditary classes of graphs.
Therefore in each equivalence class of compatibility
there can be only one
(\mathcal{H},k)
-equivalence class that is comprised of
k
-boundaried graphs which do not belong to
\mathcal{H}
.

The relevance of the
(\mathcal{H},k)
-equivalence relation for solving
\mathcal{H}
-deletion
can be seen from the observation below.

Observation 5.29
.

Consider
k
-boundaried graphs
(G_{1},X_{1},\lambda_{1})
,
(G_{2},X_{2},\lambda_{2})
,
(H,X_{3},\lambda_{3})
, such that
(G_{1},X_{1},\lambda_{1})
,
(G_{2},X_{2},\lambda_{2})
are
(\mathcal{H},k)
-equivalent and compatible with
(H,X_{3},\lambda_{3})
.
Let
S\subseteq V(H)\setminus X_{3}
.
Then
(H-S,X_{3},\lambda_{3})\oplus(G_{1},X_{1},\lambda_{1})\in\mathcal{H}
if and only if
(H-S,X_{3},\lambda_{3})\oplus(G_{2},X_{2},\lambda_{2})\in\mathcal{H}
.

The
\mathcal{H}
-membership
problem is the problem of deciding whether a given graph belongs to
\mathcal{H}
. We say that the
\mathcal{H}
-membership
problem is finite state if the relation of
(\mathcal{H},k)
-equivalence has finitely many equivalence classes for each
k
.
A
k
-boundaried graph
(G,X,\lambda)
is called a
minimal representative
in the relation of
(\mathcal{H},k)
-equivalence if
G\in\mathcal{H}
and there is no other
k
-boundaried graph, which is
(\mathcal{H},k)
-equivalent and has a smaller number of vertices.
We remark that in the literature it is more common to define representatives also for equivalence classes in which the underlying graphs do not belong to
\mathcal{H}
but for our purposes such a restriction is sufficient.
It will allow us to (a) avoid non-interesting corner cases and (b) exploit the properties of some classes
\mathcal{H}
to bound the number of minimal representatives.

A family

\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{H}}_{k}
is called
(\mathcal{H},k)
-representative if it contains a minimal representative from each
(\mathcal{H},k)
-equivalence class where the underlying graphs belong
\mathcal{H}
. It will not matter how the ties are broken.
A family
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{H}}_{\leq k}
is called
(\mathcal{H},\leq k)
-representative if it is a union of
(\mathcal{H},t)
-representative families for all
t\in[k]
.
We define
\texttt{vol}(\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{H}}_{\leq k})=\sum_{{R}\in\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{H}}_{\leq k}}|V({R})|
.
Note that even though there may be many
ways to construct such a family, the sum above is well-defined,
as well as the size of the family.
Let us note that we can effectively test
(\mathcal{H},k)
-equivalence as long as some
(\mathcal{H},k)
-representative family is provided.

Observation 5.30
.

Let
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{H}}_{k}
be an
(\mathcal{H},k)
-representative family.
Suppose that
k
-boundaried graphs
(G_{1},X_{1},\lambda_{1})
,
(G_{2},X_{2},\lambda_{2})
are compatible and for every compatible
k
-boundaried graph
(G_{3},X_{3},\lambda_{3})
from
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{H}}_{k}
it holds that
(G_{1},X_{1},\lambda_{1})\oplus(G_{3},X_{3},\lambda_{3})\in\mathcal{H}\Longleftrightarrow(G_{2},X_{2},\lambda_{2})\oplus(G_{3},X_{3},\lambda_{3})\in\mathcal{H}
.
Then
(G_{1},X_{1},\lambda_{1})
,
(G_{2},X_{2},\lambda_{2})
are
(\mathcal{H},k)
-equivalent.

We will be interested in upper bounding the maximal number of vertices of a graph in an
(\mathcal{H},\leq k)
-representative family
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{H}}_{\leq k}
;
we will denote this quantity by
r_{\mathcal{H}}(k)
.
Since there are
2^{\mathcal{O}((r_{\mathcal{H}}(k))^{2})}
different graphs on
r_{\mathcal{H}}(k)
vertices,
this gives an immediate bound on
|\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{H}}_{\leq k}|
and
\texttt{vol}(\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{H}}_{\leq k})
.
What is more, we can construct an
(\mathcal{H},\leq k)
-representative family within the same running time.

Lemma 5.31
.

Consider a polynomially recognizable graph class
\mathcal{H}
such that
\mathcal{H}
-membership
is finite state and there is a time-constructible
4
4

4

A function
r\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
is time-constructible if there exists a Turing machine that, given a string
1^{k}
, outputs the binary representation of
r(k)
in time
\mathcal{O}(r(k))
. We add this condition so we can assume that the value of
r(k)
is known to the algorithm. All functions of the form
r(k)=\alpha\cdot k^{c}
, where
\alpha,c
are positive integers, are time-constructible.
function
r(k)\geq k
, such that
for every integer
k
and for every
t
-boundaried graph, where
t\in[k]
, there exists an
(\mathcal{H},t)
-equivalent
t
-boundaried graph on at most
r(k)
vertices.
Then there exists an algorithm that, given an integer
k
, runs in time
2^{\mathcal{O}((r(k))^{2})}

and returns an
(\mathcal{H},\leq k)
-representative family.

Proof.

Consider a process in which we begin from an empty graph on
n
vertices, fix
t
boundary vertices, and choose an adjacency matrix determining which pairs of vertices share an edge.
By iterating over all possible adjacency matrices, we can generate all
t
-boundaried graphs on
n
vertices.

We enumerate all
2^{\mathcal{O}((r(k))^{2})}

t
-boundaried graphs from
\mathcal{H}
on at most
r(k)
vertices, for all
t\in[k]
.
For each pair of
t
-boundaried graphs which are compatible, we perform gluing and check (in time polynomial in
r(k)
) whether the obtained graph belongs to
\mathcal{H}
.
By Observation
5.30
, this provides sufficient information to divide the generated
t
-boundaried graphs into
(\mathcal{H},t)
-equivalence classes.
We construct an
(\mathcal{H},t)
-representative family
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{H}}_{t}
, for each
t\in[k]
,
by picking any minimal representative from each class; as noted before, it does not matter how the ties are broken.
∎

5.3.2
Dynamic programming with
A
-exhaustive families

We move on to designing a meta-algorithm
for solving
\mathcal{H}
-deletion
parameterized by
\mathcal{H}
-treewidth.
Let
S
be an optimal solution and
t
be a node
in a tree
\mathcal{H}
-decomposition.
We will associate some tri-separation
(A,X,B)
with
t
,
where
A
stands for the set of vertices introduced below
t
and
|X|\leq k
for
k-1
being the width of the decomposition.
The main idea is to consider all potential tri-separations
(A_{S},X_{S},B_{S})
that may be obtained after removing
S
.
We enumerate all the non-equivalent
k
-boundaried graphs which may be isomorphic with
(G[B_{S}\cup X_{S}],X_{S})
and seek a minimal deletion set within
A
so that the resulting graph belongs to
\mathcal{H}
.
We will show that it suffices to consider all the representatives in the relation of
(\mathcal{H},k)
-equivalence to construct an
A
-exhaustive family of partial solutions.
We shall use the following problem to describe a subroutine for
finding such deletion sets in the leaves of the decomposition.

Disjoint
\mathcal{H}
-deletion

Parameter:

s,\ell

Input:
A graph
G
, integers
s,\ell
, and a subset
U\subseteq V(G)
of size at most
\ell
, which is an
\mathcal{H}
-deletion set in
G
.

Task:
Either return a minimum-size
\mathcal{H}
-deletion set
S\subseteq V(G)\setminus U
or conclude that no such set of size at most
s
exists.

We introduce two parameters
s,\ell
that control the solution size and the size of the set
U
.
In the majority of cases we can adapt known algorithms for
\mathcal{H}
-deletion
to solve
Disjoint
\mathcal{H}
-deletion
even without the assumptions on the size and structure of
U
.
It is however convenient to impose such requirements on
U
because (1) this captures the subproblem we need to solve in Lemma
5.32
and (2) this allows us to adapt the known algorithm for
\mathcal{H}=\mathsf{planar}
(Theorem
5.41
).

The following lemma shows that the requirement of having undeletable vertices is easy to overcome if
\mathcal{H}
is closed under the addition of true twins. We say that vertices
u,v\in V(G)
are
true-twins
if
N_{G}[u]=N_{G}[v]
.
Note that in the following lemma the value of parameter
\ell
is insignificant.

Lemma 5.32
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a graph class closed under the addition of true twins and such that
\mathcal{H}
-deletion
parameterized by the solution size
s
can be solved in
f(n,s)
time.
Then the problem
Disjoint
\mathcal{H}
-deletion
can be solved in
f(s\cdot n,s)
time.

Proof.

Let
G^{\prime}
be the graph obtained from
G
by making
s+1
new true-twin copies of every vertex in the undeletable set
U
. Then for every set
S\subseteq V(G)\setminus U
for which
G-S
is a member of
\mathcal{H}
, the graph
G^{\prime}-S
is also in
\mathcal{H}
. Conversely, any minimum-size set
S\subseteq V(G^{\prime})
of size at most
s
for which
G^{\prime}-S
in
\mathcal{H}
does not contain any copies of vertices in
U
, as the budget of
s
vertices is insufficient to contain all copies of a vertex, while a solution that avoids one copy of a vertex can avoid all copies, since members of
\mathcal{H}
are closed under the addition of true twins. Consequently, an optimal solution in
G^{\prime}
of size at most
s
is disjoint from
U
, and is also a solution in the induced subgraph
G
of
G^{\prime}
. Hence to compute a set
S
as desired, it suffices to compute an optimal
\mathcal{H}
-deletion set in
G^{\prime}
if there is one of size at most
s
, which can be done by assumption.
∎

Lemma 5.33
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{H}
-membership
is finite state,
\mathcal{H}
is hereditary and union-closed, and
Disjoint
\mathcal{H}
-deletion
admits an algorithm with running time
f(s,\ell)\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.
Then there is an algorithm that, given a tri-separation
(A,X,B)
of order
k
in a graph
G
such that
G[A]\in\mathcal{H}
, and an
(\mathcal{H},\leq k)
-representative family
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{H}}_{\leq k}
, runs in time
2^{k}\cdot f(k,r_{\mathcal{H}}(k))\cdot\texttt{vol}(\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{H}}_{\leq k})^{\mathcal{O}(1)}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
and outputs a family
\mathcal{S}
of size at most
2^{k}\cdot|\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{H}}_{\leq k}|
that is
A
-exhaustive for
\mathcal{H}
-deletion
on
G
.

Proof.

Initialize
\mathcal{S}=\emptyset
.
For each subset
X^{\prime}\subseteq X
, fix an arbitrary bijection
\lambda\colon[|X^{\prime}|]\to X^{\prime}
and consider the graph
G-(X\setminus X^{\prime})
.
It admits a tri-separation
(A,X^{\prime},B)
.
For each representative
{R}\in\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{H}}_{t}
, where
t=|X^{\prime}|
, which is compatible with
(G[B\cup X^{\prime}],X^{\prime},\lambda)
,
we perform the gluing operation

G_{R}=(G[A\cup X^{\prime}],X^{\prime},\lambda)\oplus{R}

and execute the algorithm for
Disjoint
\mathcal{H}
-deletion
on
G_{R}
with the set of undeletable vertices
U=V(G_{R})\setminus A
and parameters
(k,r_{\mathcal{H}}(k))
.
In other words, we seek
a minimum-size deletion set
A^{\prime}\subseteq A
of size at most
k
.
If such a set is found, we add it to
\mathcal{S}
.
Note that
U
is indeed an
\mathcal{H}
-deletion set in
G_{R}

because
G_{R}-U=G[A]
which belongs to
\mathcal{H}
by the assumption.
By the definition of an
(\mathcal{H},\leq k)
-representative family we have that
|U|\leq|V(R)|\leq r_{\mathcal{H}}(k)
, so the created instance meets the specification of
Disjoint
\mathcal{H}
-deletion
.

The constructed family clearly has size at most
2^{k}\cdot|\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{H}}_{\leq k}|
.
The running time can be upper bounded by
2^{k}\cdot\sum_{{R}\in\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{H}}_{\leq k}}f(k,r_{\mathcal{H}}(k))(n+|V({R})|)^{\mathcal{O}(1)}=2^{k}\cdot f(k,r_{\mathcal{H}}(k))\cdot\texttt{vol}(\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{H}}_{\leq k})^{\mathcal{O}(1)}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.
It remains to show that
\mathcal{S}
is indeed
A
-exhaustive.

Consider a minimum-size solution
S
to
\mathcal{H}
-deletion
on
G
. Define
S_{A},S_{X},S_{B}
as
S\cap A,S\cap X,S\cap B
, respectively, and let
X^{\prime}:=X\setminus S_{X}
,
|X^{\prime}|=t
.
Fix an arbitrary bijection
\lambda\colon[t]\to X^{\prime}
.
Since
\mathcal{H}
is union-closed and
G[A]\in\mathcal{H}
, by Lemma
5.1
we know that
|S_{A}|\leq k
.
The graph
G[B\cup X^{\prime}]-S_{B}
is an induced subgraph of
G-S
so it belongs to
\mathcal{H}
.
The set
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{H}}_{t}
contains a
t
-boundaried graph
R
that is
(\mathcal{H},t)
-equivalent to
(G[B\cup X^{\prime}]-S_{B},X^{\prime},\lambda)
.
By Observation
5.29
,

S_{A}
is an
\mathcal{H}
-deletion set for
(G[A\cup X^{\prime}],X^{\prime},\lambda)\oplus R
.
As
(G[A\cup X^{\prime}],X^{\prime},\lambda)\oplus R
contains an
\mathcal{H}
-deletion set within
A
of size at most
k
, some set
S^{\prime}_{A}
with this property has been added to
\mathcal{S}
.
Furthermore,
S^{\prime}_{A}
is a minimum-size solution, so
|S^{\prime}_{A}|\leq|S_{A}|
.
Again by Observation
5.29
,
S^{\prime}_{A}
is an
\mathcal{H}
-deletion set for
(G[A\cup X^{\prime}],X^{\prime},\lambda)\oplus(G[B\cup X^{\prime}]-S_{B},X^{\prime},\lambda)
.
It means that
S^{\prime}=(S\setminus A)\cup S^{\prime}_{A}=S_{B}\cup S_{X}\cup S^{\prime}_{A}
is an
\mathcal{H}
-deletion set in
G
and
|S^{\prime}|\leq|S|
, which finishes the proof.
∎

The next step is to propagate the partial solutions along the decomposition in a bottom-up manner.
As we want to grow the sets
A
for which
A
-exhaustive families are computed, we
can take advantage of Observation
5.26
and Lemma
5.27
.
However, after processing several nodes, the size of
A
-exhaustive families computed this way can become arbitrarily large.
In order to circumvent this, we shall prune the
A
-exhaustive families after each step.

Lemma 5.34
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{H}
-membership
is finite state and graphs in the class
\mathcal{H}
can be recognized in polynomial time.
There is an algorithm that, given a tri-separation
(A,X,B)
of order
k
in a graph
G
, a family
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\subseteq 2^{A}
that is
A
-exhaustive for
\mathcal{H}
-
deletion
on
G
, and an
(\mathcal{H},\leq k)
-representative family
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{H}}_{\leq k}
, runs in time
2^{k}\cdot|\mathcal{S}^{\prime}|\cdot\texttt{vol}(\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{H}}_{\leq k})^{\mathcal{O}(1)}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
and outputs a family
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
of size at most
2^{k}\cdot|\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{H}}_{\leq k}|
that is
A
-exhaustive for
\mathcal{H}
-
deletion
on
G
.

Proof.

Initialize
\mathcal{S}=\emptyset
.
For each subset
X^{\prime}\subseteq X
, fix an arbitrary bijection
\lambda\colon[|X^{\prime}|]\to X^{\prime}
and consider the graph
G-(X\setminus X^{\prime})
.
It admits a tri-separation
(A,X^{\prime},B)
.
For each graph
R\in\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{H}}_{t}
, where
t=|X^{\prime}|
, which is compatible with
(G[B\cup X^{\prime}],X^{\prime},\lambda)
,
we perform the gluing operation

G_{R}=(G[A\cup X^{\prime}],X^{\prime},\lambda)\oplus R
.
Using the polynomial-time recognition algorithm we choose a minimum-size set
S_{A}\in\mathcal{S^{\prime}}
which is an
\mathcal{H}
-deletion set for
G_{R}
, if one exists, and add it to
\mathcal{S}
.

We construct at most
2^{k}\cdot|\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{H}}_{\leq k}|
graphs
G_{R}
. For each graph
G_{R}
we add at most one set to
\mathcal{S}
and spend
|\mathcal{S^{\prime}}|\cdot(n+|R|)^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
time. In total, we perform at most
2^{k}\cdot|\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{H}}_{\leq k}|\cdot|\mathcal{S^{\prime}}|\cdot\sum_{{R}\in\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{H}}_{\leq k}}(n+|V({R})|)^{\mathcal{O}(1)}=2^{k}\cdot|\mathcal{S^{\prime}}|\cdot\texttt{vol}(\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{H}}_{\leq k})^{\mathcal{O}(1)}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
operations.
It remains to show that
\mathcal{S}
is indeed
A
-exhaustive.

Consider a minimum-size solution
S
to
\mathcal{H}
-deletion
on
G
. Define
S_{A},S_{X},S_{B}
as
S\cap A,S\cap X,S\cap B
, respectively, and let
X^{\prime}:=X\setminus S_{X}
,
|X^{\prime}|=t
. Since
\mathcal{S^{\prime}}
is
A
-exhaustive on
G
, there exists
\widehat{S}_{A}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
such that
\widehat{S}=(S\setminus A)\cup\widehat{S}_{A}
is also a minimum-size solution to
G
, implying that
|\widehat{S}_{A}|\leq|S_{A}|
.
Fix an arbitrary bijection
\lambda\colon[t]\to X^{\prime}
.
The set
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{H}}_{t}
contains a
t
-boundaried graph
R
that is
(\mathcal{H},t)
-equivalent to
(G[B\cup X^{\prime}]-S_{B},X^{\prime},\lambda)
.
By Observation
5.29
, a set
A^{\prime}\subseteq A
is an
\mathcal{H}
-deletion set for
G^{\prime}=(G[A\cup X^{\prime}],X^{\prime},\lambda)\oplus(G[B\cup X^{\prime}]-S_{B},X^{\prime},\lambda)
if and only if
A^{\prime}
is
an
\mathcal{H}
-deletion set for
(G[A\cup X^{\prime}],X^{\prime},\lambda)\oplus R
.
Since
G^{\prime}-\widehat{S}_{A}=G-\widehat{S}\in\mathcal{H}
, we know that
\widehat{S}_{A}\in\mathcal{S^{\prime}}
is such a set. Hence by the construction above, there exists some (possibly different)
S^{\prime}_{A}\in\mathcal{S}
with this property and minimum size;
hence
|S^{\prime}_{A}|\leq|\widehat{S}_{A}|\leq|S_{A}|
and

S^{\prime}=(S\setminus A)\cup S^{\prime}_{A}=S_{B}\cup S_{X}\cup S^{\prime}_{A}
is an
\mathcal{H}
-deletion set in
G
and
|S^{\prime}|\leq|S|
. The claim follows.
∎

We are ready to combine the presented subroutines in a general meta-algorithm to process tree
\mathcal{H}
-decompositions for every class
\mathcal{H}
which satisfies three simple conditions. The following statement formalizes and, together with
Lemma

5.31
, proves Theorem
2.1
from Section
2.2
.

Theorem 5.35
.

Suppose that the class
\mathcal{H}
satisfies the following:

1.

\mathcal{H}
is hereditary and union-closed,

2.

Disjoint
\mathcal{H}
-deletion
admits an algorithm with running time
f(s,\ell)\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
,

3.

\mathcal{H}
-membership
is finite state and there is an algorithm computing an
(\mathcal{H},\leq t)
-representative family with running time
v(t)
.

Then
\mathcal{H}
-deletion
can be solved in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(k)}\cdot f(k,r_{\mathcal{H}}(k))\cdot v(k)^{\mathcal{O}(1)}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
when given a tree
\mathcal{H}
-decomposition of width
k-1
consisting of
n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
nodes.

Proof.

First, we construct an
(\mathcal{H},\leq k)
-representative family
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{H}}_{\leq k}
in time
v(k)
.
Since the output size of the algorithm cannot exceed its running time, we have
|\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{H}}_{\leq k}|\leq\texttt{vol}(\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{H}}_{\leq k})\leq v(k)
.

The algorithm is based on a variant of dynamic programming in which bounded-size sets of partial solutions are computed, with the guarantee that at least one of the partial solutions which are stored can be completed to an optimal solution. More formally, for each node
t\in V(T)
we are going to compute (recall
\kappa,\pi
from Definition
5.7
) a set of partial solutions
\mathcal{S}_{t}\subseteq 2^{\kappa(t)}
of size at most
2^{k}\cdot|\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{H}}_{\leq k}|
which is
\kappa(t)
-exhaustive for
\mathcal{H}
-deletion
in
G
. As
\kappa(r)=V(G)
for the root node
r
by Observation
5.8
, any minimum-size set
S\in\mathcal{S}_{r}
for which
G-S\in\mathcal{H}
is an optimal solution to the problem, and the property of
\kappa(r)
-exhaustive families guarantees that one exists.
We do the computation bottom-up in the tree decomposition, using Lemma
5.34
to prune sets of partial solutions at intermediate steps to prevent them from becoming too large.

Let
(T,\chi,L)
be the given tree
\mathsf{\mathcal{H}}
-decomposition of width
k-1
. By Lemma
5.5
we may assume that the decomposition is nice and is rooted at some node
r
.
For
t\in V(T)
, define
L_{t}:=L\cap\chi(t)
. Process the nodes of
T
from bottom to top. We process a node
t
after having computed exhaustive families for all its children, as follows. Let
X_{t}:=\chi(t)\cap\pi(t)
, let
A_{t}:=\kappa(t)
and let
B_{t}:=V(G)\setminus(A_{t}\cup X_{t})
. By Observation
5.9
, the partition
(A_{t},X_{t},B_{t})
is a tri-separation of
G
. The way in which we continue processing
t
depends on the number of children it has. As
T
is a nice decomposition, node
t
has at most two children.

Leaf nodes
For a leaf node
t\in V(T)
, we construct an exhaustive family of partial solutions
\mathcal{S}_{t}\subseteq 2^{\kappa(t)}
as follows.
By Definition
3.4
, vertices of
L_{t}
do not occur in other bags than
\chi(t)
.
Because the decomposition is nice, we have
